(i.e., that both the maximal arity of the symbols and the number of record fields are bounded by
\kappa
).
The number of

I
-free descendants of any sequent
{\cal S}
is polynomial w.r.t.
|{\cal S}|+|\mathfrak{R}|
(but it is exponential w.r.t.
\kappa
).

Proof 5.20

By Lemma
5.15
, it is sufficient to prove that the total number of paths occurring in a proof tree with no application of

I
is polynomial w.r.t.
|{\cal S}|+|\mathfrak{R}|
.

The rules

R
,

W
,

V
,

E
apply with the highest priority
and yield only one branch, yielding a (unique) sequent
\phi\curlywedge\xi\vdash\psi\curlywedge\zeta
.
If
\zeta\not=\top
, then by Definition
4.43
, no other rule can be applied because these other rules apply only when the right-hand side is of the form
\psi\curlywedge\top
.
Otherwise, the rule

S
may apply (possibly several times), and eventually transforms the sequent into sequents

\phi_{i}\curlywedge\xi_{i}\vdash_{\mathfrak{R}}^{V_{i}}\psi_{i}\curlywedge\top
, where each
\psi_{i}
is a predicate atom,

\phi=\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.5}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}_{i=1}^{n}\phi_{i}
and

\psi=\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.5}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}_{i=1}^{n}\psi_{i}

(we may have
\xi_{i}\not=\xi

since the rule may interleaved
with applications of rule

W
).

If the considered sequent is not narrow, then by definition of the strategy (Condition
7
in Definition
4.43
), there is at most one application
of rule

S
;
indeed, if several rule applications are possible then only the one that is selected is considered. We thus obtain at most
|{\cal S}|
branches, each ending with a narrow sequent (because
\psi_{i}
is a predicate atom, thus
\phi_{i}\curlywedge\xi_{i}\vdash_{\mathfrak{R}}^{V_{i}}\psi_{i}
is necessarily narrow).

Now assume that
{\cal S}
is narrow and let
x_{i}
be the root of
\psi_{i}
. We have
\mathit{alloc}(\psi_{i})=\{x_{i}\}
, because
\psi_{i}
is a predicate atom.
Since
\phi_{i}*\xi_{i}\vdash_{\mathfrak{R}}^{V_{i}}\psi_{i}
is not an axiom,
we have
x_{i}\in\mathit{alloc}(\phi_{i})
. By definition of rule

S
, this entails that
x_{i}\in V_{j}
, for all
j\in\{1,\dots,i-1,i+1,\dots,n\}
.
For

S
to be applied, it is only necessary to choose how to decompose the
formula
\phi
into
\mathchoice{\scalebox{1.5}{$\displaystyle*$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\textstyle*$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptstyle*$}}{\scalebox{1.5}{$\scriptscriptstyle*$}}_{i=1}^{n}\phi_{i}
.
To this aim, we only have to associate each atom in
\phi
with a formula
\phi_{i}
, hence to associate each variable
y\in\mathit{alloc}(\phi)
(that are by definition the roots of the spatial atoms in
\phi
) to an index
i=1,\dots,n
.
By definition, the variable
x_{i}
must be associated with index
i
, as otherwise we would have
x_{i}\not\in\mathit{alloc}(\phi_{i})
and the premise

\phi_{i}*\xi_{i}\vdash_{\mathfrak{R}}^{V_{i}}\psi_{i}
would be an anti-axiom.
We then arbitrarily choose the image of each variable occurring in
{\cal V}^{\dagger}({\cal S})
.
Afterward, the image of the other variables are fixed inductively as follows.
Let
y\in\mathit{alloc}(\phi)
and assume that
y\not\in{\cal V}^{\dagger}({\cal S})\cup\{x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\}
.
If
y
occurs in some atom with root
y^{\prime}
in
\phi
and
y^{\prime}
has already been associated with
i
,
then we also associate
y
with
i
.
For the sake of contradiction, assume that
y
is associated with an index
j\not=i
.
By definition of rule

S
, this entails that
y\in V_{i}
, and since

\phi_{i}*\xi_{i}\vdash_{\mathfrak{R}}^{V_{i}}\psi_{i}
is not an anti-axiom, we deduce that
y\in\mathcal{V}(\psi_{i})
, hence

y\in\mathcal{V}(\psi)\setminus(\mathit{alloc}(\psi)\cup V)={\cal V}^{\dagger}({\cal S})
, which contradicts our assumption (we cannot have
y\in V
since
{\cal S}
would then be an axiom).
Note that, for all variables
y\in\mathit{alloc}(\phi)\setminus\{x_{1},\dots,x_{n}\}
,
we have
x_{i}\rightarrow_{\phi}^{*}y
, for some
i=1,\dots,n
, because otherwise
{\cal S}
would be an anti-axiom.
Hence all such variables
y
must be eventually associated with some indice
i=1,\dots,n
.
Since
{\cal S}
is narrow,
\mathit{card}({\cal V}^{\dagger}({\cal S}))\leq\kappa
. Consequently,
there exist at most
|{\cal S}|^{\kappa}
possible applications of rule

S
, each yielding
n\leq|{\cal S}|
branches. We thus get a total of
at most
|{\cal S}|^{\kappa+1}
branches.

Afterwards, rule

U
applies, yielding at most
|\mathfrak{R}|
premises in each branch.
Then

C
applies on each leaf sequent
{\cal S}^{\prime}
, and by Lemma
5.17

we get at most
|\mathfrak{R}|\cdot\mathtt{NbDisEq}({\cal S}^{\prime})^{\kappa}
branches.
The variables in
{\cal S}^{\prime}
either occur in
{\cal S}
or are introduced by the rule

U
.
Since each application of

U
introduces at most
\mathit{record}_{\mathit{max}}(\mathfrak{R})
variables, we get
\mathtt{NbDisEq}({\cal S}^{\prime})\leq(|{\cal S}|+\mathit{record}_{\mathit{max}}(\mathfrak{R}))^{2}
.

We derive the main result of this section:

Theorem 5.21

The root sequent of every (possibly infinite) fully expanded proof tree is valid.

Proof 5.22

Let
{\cal S}
be a non-valid sequent, with a counter-model
(\mathfrak{s},\mathfrak{h})
.
By Lemma
4.32
,
{\cal S}
is not an axiom, hence by definition of a fully expanded proof tree, it admits successors.
By Lemmata
4.26
and
4.29
, one of these successors must be non-valid and must admit a counter-model
(\mathfrak{s},\mathfrak{h}^{\prime})
, with
\mathit{card}(\mathfrak{h}^{\prime})\leq\mathit{card}(\mathfrak{h})
, and if the rule that applies on
{\cal S}
is

S
, then
\mathit{card}(\mathfrak{h}^{\prime})<\mathit{card}(\mathfrak{h})
.
Starting from the root of the tree,
we thus obtain (if this root is not valid) an infinite path
{\cal S}_{0},\dots,{\cal S}_{n},\dots,
such that
all the
{\cal S}_{i}
admit a counter-model
(\mathfrak{s}_{i},\mathfrak{h}_{i})
,
\mathit{card}(\mathfrak{h}_{i+1})\leq\mathit{card}(\mathfrak{h}_{i})
and
if

S
is applies on
{\cal S}_{i}
then
\mathit{card}(\mathfrak{h}_{i+1})<\mathit{card}(\mathfrak{h}_{i})
.
Since
\mathit{card}(\mathfrak{h})
is finite this entails that
there exists
i\geq 0
such that rule

S
does not apply on
{\cal S}_{j}
, for all
j\geq i
.
By Lemma
5.2
, this entails that there exists
k
such that

I
does not apply on
{\cal S}_{j}
, for all
j\geq k
. Thus

{\cal S}_{k}
admits an infinite number of

I
-free descendants, which contradicts Lemma
5.19
.

5.2
Completeness

We now establish completeness, i.e., we prove that every valid sequent admits a fully expanded tree.
To this aim, we prove that for every valid sequent, there exists a rule application yielding valid premises.
Lemma
5.32
handles the case of rule

S
,
and Lemma
5.34
handles all the other rules. We begin by establishing several preliminary results.
First, we note that the truth value of a formula in a structure is not dependent on the
names
of the locations: these locations can be freely renamed, provided the relations between the variables are preserved, and provided allocated locations are not mapped to the same image. This result will be useful to construct copies of models when needed in forthcoming proofs.
More formally, we introduce a notion of a
\mathfrak{U}
-mapping
and state some conditions ensuring that the application of such
\mathfrak{U}
-mappings on a structure preserves the truth value of a formula.

Definition 5.23

A
\mathfrak{U}
-mapping is a function
\nu
mapping every element of
\mathfrak{U}_{\mathtt{s}}
to an element of
\mathfrak{U}_{{\mathtt{s}}}
.
Let
\mathfrak{h}
be a heap.
If
\nu
is injective on
\mathit{dom}(\mathfrak{h})
, then we denote by
\nu(\mathfrak{h})
the heap with domain
\nu(\mathit{dom}(\mathfrak{h}))
, such that for all
\ell_{0}\in\mathit{dom}(\mathfrak{h})
, with

\mathfrak{h}(\ell_{0})=(\ell_{1},\dots,\ell_{n})
,
we have

\nu(\mathfrak{h})(\nu(\ell_{0}))=(\nu(\ell_{1}),\dots,\nu(\ell_{n}))
.

Lemma 5.24

Let
\lambda
be a symbolic heap, let
(\mathfrak{s},\mathfrak{h})
be an
\mathfrak{R}
-model of
\lambda
and let
\nu

be a
\mathfrak{U}
-mapping that is injective on
\mathfrak{s}(\mathcal{V}(\lambda))\cup\mathit{dom}(\mathfrak{h})
. Then
(\nu\circ\mathfrak{s},\nu(\mathfrak{h}))\models_{\mathfrak{R}}\lambda
.

Proof 5.25

The proof is by induction on the satisfiability relation. The result is established also for spatial formulas and pure formulas.

•

If
\lambda
is an equation
x\approx y
, then
\mathfrak{s}(x)=\mathfrak{s}(y)
, hence

\nu(\mathfrak{s}(x))=\nu(\mathfrak{s}(y))
and

(\nu\circ\mathfrak{s},\nu(\mathfrak{h}))\models_{\mathfrak{R}}\lambda
.

•

If
\lambda
is a disequation
x\not\approx y
, then
\mathfrak{s}(x)\not=\mathfrak{s}(y)
, and since
\nu
is injective on
\mathfrak{s}(\mathcal{V}(\lambda))
we get

\nu(\mathfrak{s}(x))\not=\nu(\mathfrak{s}(y))
and

(\nu\circ\mathfrak{s},\nu(\mathfrak{h}))\models_{\mathfrak{R}}\lambda
.

•

If
\lambda=y_{0}\mapsto(y_{1},\dots,y_{n})
then we must have
\mathfrak{h}=\{(\mathfrak{s}(y_{0}),\dots,\mathfrak{s}(y_{n}))\}
,
which entails that

\nu(\mathfrak{h})
is
{\{(\nu(\mathfrak{s}(y_{0})),\dots,\nu(\mathfrak{s}(y_{n})))\}}

and that

(\nu\circ\mathfrak{s},\nu(\mathfrak{h}))\models_{\mathfrak{R}}\lambda
.

•

If
\lambda=\phi\curlywedge\xi
(or
\lambda=\phi\wedge\xi
) then
we have
(\mathfrak{s},\mathfrak{h})\models_{\mathfrak{R}}\phi

and

(\mathfrak{s},\mathfrak{h})\models_{\mathfrak{R}}\xi
.
By the induction hypothesis, we get

(\nu\circ\mathfrak{s},\nu(\mathfrak{h}))\models_{\mathfrak{R}}\phi

and

(\nu\circ\mathfrak{s},\nu(\mathfrak{h}))\models_{\mathfrak{R}}\xi
, thus

(\nu\circ\mathfrak{s},\nu(\mathfrak{h}))\models_{\mathfrak{R}}\lambda
.

•

If
\lambda=\phi_{1}*\phi_{2}
then there exists disjoint heaps

\mathfrak{h}_{i}
(for
i=1,2
) such that

\mathfrak{h}=\mathfrak{h}_{1}\uplus\mathfrak{h}_{2}
and

(\mathfrak{s},\mathfrak{h}_{i})\models_{\mathfrak{R}}\phi_{i}
. Since
\mathfrak{s}(\mathcal{V}(\phi_{i}))\cup\mathit{dom}(\mathfrak{h}_{i})\subseteq\mathfrak{s}(\mathcal{V}(\lambda))\cup\mathit{dom}(\mathfrak{h})
, by the induction hypothesis we have

(\nu\circ\mathfrak{s},\nu(\mathfrak{h}_{i}))\models_{\mathfrak{R}}\phi_{i}
(for
i=1,2
).
But
\nu(\mathfrak{h}_{1})
and
\nu(\mathfrak{h}_{2})
must be disjoint since

\mathit{dom}(\mathfrak{h}_{1})\cap\mathit{dom}(\mathfrak{h}_{2})=\emptyset
and
\nu
is injective, therefore

(\nu\circ\mathfrak{s},\nu(\mathfrak{h}))\models_{\mathfrak{R}}\lambda
.

•

If
\lambda
is a predicate atom, then we have

\lambda\Leftarrow_{\mathfrak{R}}\gamma
and

(\mathfrak{s}^{\prime},\mathfrak{h})\models_{\mathfrak{R}}\gamma
, for some associate
\mathfrak{s}^{\prime}
of
\mathfrak{s}
w.r.t.
\mathcal{V}(\gamma)\setminus\mathcal{V}(\lambda)
.
Since the rules in
\mathfrak{R}
are
\mathtt{P}
-rules, for all variables
x\in\mathcal{V}(\gamma)\setminus\mathcal{V}(\lambda)
, we have
x\in\mathit{alloc}(\gamma)
thus
\mathfrak{s}^{\prime}(x)\in\mathit{dom}(\mathfrak{h})
by Lemma
4.2
.
This entails that
\nu
is injective on
\mathfrak{s}^{\prime}(\mathcal{V}(\gamma))\cup\mathit{dom}(\mathfrak{h})
, and
by the induction hypothesis we get

(\nu\circ\mathfrak{s}^{\prime},\nu(\mathfrak{h}))\models_{\mathfrak{R}}\xi
.
Moreover, it is clear that
\nu(\mathfrak{s}^{\prime})
is an associate of
\nu(\mathfrak{s})
w.r.t.
\mathcal{V}(\gamma)\setminus\mathcal{V}(\lambda)
, thus

(\nu\circ\mathfrak{s},\nu(\mathfrak{h}))\models_{\mathfrak{R}}\lambda
.

This result entails that for every set
U
that is sufficiently large, counter-models may always be renamed, so that all locations occur in
U
:

Corollary 5.26

Let
U
be an infinite subset of
\mathfrak{U}_{{\mathtt{s}}}
.
Any non-valid and equality-free sequent
\lambda\vdash_{\mathfrak{R}}^{V}\gamma
admits a counter-model

(\mathfrak{s},\mathfrak{h})
such that
\mathit{dom}(\mathfrak{h})\cup\mathfrak{s}(\mathcal{V}_{\mathtt{loc}})\subseteq U
.

Proof 5.27

It suffices to consider any counter-model of
\gamma
and apply a bijective
\mathfrak{U}
-mapping (or any
\mathfrak{U}
-mapping that is injective on
\mathfrak{s}(\mathcal{V}(\lambda))\cup\mathit{dom}(\mathfrak{h})
)
that maps all locations
\ell\in\mathfrak{U}_{\mathtt{loc}}
to an element in
U
.

The first step toward establishing completeness is to show that rule

S
always applies (on a valid sequent) if the right-hand side of the sequent contains several spatial formulas (assuming that the rules of higher priority do not apply). This is essential because the strategy in Definition
4.43
forbids the application of the other rules in this case. The difficulty is that, of course,

S
cannot be applied arbitrarily: we are required to obtain valid premises. To this purpose we show that the heap decomposition of the left-hand side (
\phi_{1}*\phi_{2}
) matches that of the right-hand side (
\psi_{1}*\psi_{2}
). This will be proven thanks to the next lemma. Intuitively, this lemmas states that, under some additional conditions, if a structure
(\mathfrak{s},\mathfrak{h}_{1}\uplus\mathfrak{h}_{2})
validates a formula
\phi_{1}*\phi_{2}
, then we may deduce that
each structure
(\mathfrak{s},\mathfrak{h}_{i})
validates
\phi_{i}
, when all the variables allocated by
\phi_{i}
are in the domain of
\mathfrak{h}_{i}
.

Lemma 5.28

Let
\phi_{i},\psi_{i}
(for
i=1,2
) be spatial formulas, with
\mathit{alloc}(\psi_{1}*\psi_{2})\subseteq\mathit{alloc}(\phi_{1}*\phi_{2})
.
Let
\mathfrak{h}_{1}
and
\mathfrak{h}_{2}
be disjoint heaps such that
(\mathfrak{s},\mathfrak{h}_{1}\uplus\mathfrak{h}_{2})\models_{\mathfrak{R}}\phi_{1}*\phi_{2}
and
(\mathfrak{s},\mathfrak{h}_{i})\models_{\mathfrak{R}}\psi_{i}
, for
i=1,2
.
If
(\mathit{dom}(\mathfrak{h}_{1})\cup\mathit{dom}(\mathfrak{h}_{2}))\cap\mathfrak{s}(\mathcal{V})\subseteq\mathfrak{s}(\mathit{alloc}(\phi_{1}*\phi_{2}))

and
\mathfrak{s}(\mathit{alloc}(\phi_{i}))\subseteq\mathit{dom}(\mathfrak{h}_{i})
for
i=1,2
,
then
(\mathfrak{s},\mathfrak{h}_{i})\models_{\mathfrak{R}}\phi_{i}
, for
i=1,2
.

Proof 5.29

By definition, since
(\mathfrak{s},\mathfrak{h}_{1}\uplus\mathfrak{h}_{2})\models_{\mathfrak{R}}\phi_{1}*\phi_{2}
, there exist disjoint heaps
\widehat{\mathfrak{h}}_{1},\widehat{\mathfrak{h}}_{2}
such that

(\mathfrak{s},\widehat{\mathfrak{h}}_{i})\models_{\mathfrak{R}}\phi_{i}
, for all
i=1,2
,
and
\mathfrak{h}_{1}\uplus\mathfrak{h}_{2}=\widehat{\mathfrak{h}}_{1}\uplus\widehat{\mathfrak{h}}_{2}
.
We prove that
\widehat{\mathfrak{h}}_{i}=\mathfrak{h}_{i}
for
i=1,2
.
Since
\widehat{\mathfrak{h}}_{1}\uplus\widehat{\mathfrak{h}}_{2}=\mathfrak{h}_{1}\uplus\mathfrak{h}_{2}

and
\widehat{\mathfrak{h}}_{1}
and
\widehat{\mathfrak{h}}_{2}
are disjoint,
it is sufficient to prove that
\mathit{dom}(\mathfrak{h}_{i})\subseteq\mathit{dom}(\widehat{\mathfrak{h}}_{i})
, for
i=1,2
.
By symmetry, we prove the result for
i=1
.
Assume, for the sake of contradiction, that
\ell\in\mathit{dom}(\mathfrak{h}_{1})
and

\ell\not\in\mathit{dom}(\widehat{\mathfrak{h}}_{1})
.
By Proposition
4.9
, since
(\mathfrak{s},\mathfrak{h}_{1})\models_{\mathfrak{R}}\psi_{1}

and
\ell\in\mathit{dom}(\mathfrak{h}_{1})\subseteq\mathit{ref}(\mathfrak{h}_{1})
, there exists
y\in\mathit{alloc}(\psi_{1})
such that

\mathfrak{s}(y)\rightarrow_{\mathfrak{h}_{1}}^{*}\ell
, thus there is a sequence of locations

\ell_{0},\dots,\ell_{n}
with
\ell_{0}=\mathfrak{s}(y)
,
\ell=\ell_{n}
and

\ell_{i}\rightarrow_{\mathfrak{h}_{1}}\ell_{i+1}
, for all
i=0,\dots,n-1
.
Let
k
be the smallest index such that
\ell_{k}\not\in\mathit{dom}(\widehat{\mathfrak{h}}_{1})
.
Note that
\ell_{i}\in\mathit{dom}(\mathfrak{h}_{1})
, for all
i\leq k
and that

y\in\mathit{alloc}(\phi_{1})
.
Indeed,
y\in\mathit{alloc}(\psi_{1})\subseteq\mathit{alloc}(\phi_{1})\cup\mathit{alloc}(\phi_{2})
by the hypothesis of the lemma, and if

y\in\mathit{alloc}(\phi_{2})
, then we get (again by the hypothesis of the lemma)

\mathfrak{s}(y)\in\mathit{dom}(\mathfrak{h}_{2})
, which contradicts the fact that
\ell_{0}=\mathfrak{s}(y)\in\mathit{dom}(\mathfrak{h}_{1})
, as
\mathfrak{h}_{1}
and
\mathfrak{h}_{2}
are disjoint.
By Lemma
4.2
, we deduce that
\ell_{0}=\mathfrak{s}(y)\in\mathit{dom}(\widehat{\mathfrak{h}}_{1})
since
y\in\mathit{alloc}(\phi_{1})
and
(\mathfrak{s},\widehat{\mathfrak{h}}_{1})\models_{\mathfrak{R}}\phi_{1}
. Thus
k>0
, and necessarily
\ell_{k}\in\mathit{ref}(\widehat{\mathfrak{h}}_{1})
(since
\ell_{k-1}\in\mathit{dom}(\widehat{\mathfrak{h}}_{1})
, by minimality of
k
).
We deduce that
\ell_{k}\in\mathit{ref}(\widehat{\mathfrak{h}}_{1})\setminus\mathit{dom}(\widehat{\mathfrak{h}}_{1})
.
By Lemma
4.11
, this entails that
\ell_{k}=\mathfrak{s}(x)
for some
x\in\mathcal{V}(\phi_{1})
, as
(\mathfrak{s},\widehat{\mathfrak{h}}_{1})\models\phi_{1}
.
By the hypothesis of the lemma, we may assume that
x\in\mathit{alloc}(\phi_{1}*\phi_{2})
, since
\ell_{k}\in\mathit{dom}(\mathfrak{h}_{1})
and
(\mathit{dom}(\mathfrak{h}_{1})\cup\mathit{dom}(\mathfrak{h}_{2}))\cap\mathfrak{s}(\mathcal{V})\subseteq\mathfrak{s}(\mathit{alloc}(\phi_{1}*\phi_{2}))
.
However, we cannot have
x\in\mathit{alloc}(\phi_{1})
because otherwise we would have
\mathfrak{s}(x)\in\mathit{dom}(\widehat{\mathfrak{h}}_{1})
by Lemma
4.2
, as
(\mathfrak{s},\widehat{\mathfrak{h}}_{1})\models_{\mathfrak{R}}\phi_{1}
. We cannot have

x\in\mathit{alloc}(\phi_{2})
either,
since otherwise we would have
\mathfrak{s}(x)\in\mathit{dom}(\mathfrak{h}_{2})
, as
\mathfrak{s}(\mathit{alloc}(\phi_{2}))\subseteq\mathit{dom}(\mathfrak{h}_{2})
by the hypothesis of the lemma, hence
\mathfrak{s}(x)\not\in\mathit{dom}(\mathfrak{h}_{1})
because
\mathfrak{h}_{1}
and
\mathfrak{h}_{2}
are disjoint.
Thus we obtain a contradiction.

We then derive the result about rule

S
. The main issue is that we have to take into account the fact that the strategy “blocks” some applications of

S
, if the sequent is not narrow (see Condition
5
in Definition
4.43
). We show that blocked applications do not yield valid premises. To illustrate the difficulties that arise when applying

S
, we provide the examples below. The first one illustrates the importance of the fact that each predicate allocates at most one parameter.

Example 5.30

Consider the sequent
p(x,y)\vdash_{\mathfrak{R}}^{\emptyset}q(x,y)*r(y)
, with the rules:

p(x,y)

\Leftarrow

x\mapsto(y)*p^{\prime}(y)

q(x,y)

\Leftarrow

x\mapsto(y)

p^{\prime}(y)

\Leftarrow

y\mapsto()

r(y)

\Leftarrow

y\mapsto()

Note that the rules of
p
are not
\mathtt{P}
-rules, as both
x
and
y
are allocated by
p(x,y)
. Here, although the sequent is indeed valid, no application of

S
yields valid premises: to show that the sequent is valid, one has first to unfold
p(x,y)
once, before applying

S
.
In our context, such a situation cannot arise as each predicate atom allocates only one of its parameters, namely its root.
As we shall see, this entails that every decomposition of the right-hand side of the sequent necessarily corresponds to some (purely syntactic) decomposition of the left-hand side.

The next example illustrates the importance of determinism.

Example 5.31

Consider the sequent
{\cal S}:p(x,y)*q(y)*p(z,y)\vdash_{\mathfrak{R}}^{\emptyset}p^{\prime}(x,y)*q^{\prime}(z,y)
, with the rules:

p(x,y)

\Leftarrow

x\mapsto(y)

q(y)

\Leftarrow

y\mapsto(z)*q(z)

q(y)

\Leftarrow

y\mapsto()

q(y)

\Leftarrow

y\mapsto(y)

p^{\prime}(x,y)

\Leftarrow

x\mapsto(z)*p^{\prime}(z,y)

q^{\prime}(x,y)

\Leftarrow

x\mapsto(z)*q^{\prime}(z,y)

p^{\prime}(x,y)

\Leftarrow

x\mapsto(y)

q^{\prime}(x,y)

\Leftarrow

x\mapsto(y)

p^{\prime}(x,y)

\Leftarrow

x\mapsto()

q^{\prime}(x,y)

\Leftarrow

x\mapsto(x)

Intuitively,
q(y)
denotes a list starting at
y
and ending either with an empty tuple or with a loop on its last element, whereas
p^{\prime}(x,y)
(resp.
q^{\prime}(x,y)
) denotes a list starting at
x
and ending either with
y
or with an empty tuple (resp. with a loop on the last element). These rules are not deterministic (for instance there is an overlap between the rules
p^{\prime}(x,y)\Leftarrow x\mapsto(z)*p^{\prime}(z,y)
and
p^{\prime}(x,y)\Leftarrow x\mapsto(y)
).
Although the sequent
{\cal S}
is valid, there is no application of

S
that yields valid premises. Indeed, none of the sequents
p(x,y)*q(y)\vdash_{\mathfrak{R}}^{\emptyset}p^{\prime}(x,y)
, or
q(y)*p(z,y)\vdash_{\mathfrak{R}}^{\emptyset}q^{\prime}(z,y)
are valid.
Here

S
cannot be applied before the entire list
q(y)
is unfolded, as the decision to group
q(y)
with
p(x,y)
or
p(y,z)
cannot be made before the last cell in
q(y)
is known. This would yield an infinite proof tree (with infinitely many branches).
The fact that the rules are deterministic prevents such a behavior to occur.

Lemma 5.32

Let
{\cal S}:\phi\curlywedge\xi\vdash_{\mathfrak{R}}^{V}(\psi_{1}*\psi_{2})\curlywedge\top
be a valid sequent
where
\psi_{i}\not=\mathit{emp}
for
i=1,2
and
\xi
is a conjunction of disequations.
If
{\cal S}
is not an axiom, then
there exists an application of

S
with conclusion
{\cal S}

for which all the premises are valid.

Proof 5.33

To prove that

S
applies on
{\cal S}
, it is necessary to show that
\phi
is of the form
\phi_{1}*\phi_{2}
. However
\phi_{i}
cannot be chosen arbitrarily, since the premises have to be valid.
To construct
\phi_{i}
, we shall construct a model of
\phi
of a particular form, get a decomposition of the heap of this model from the formula
\psi_{1}*\psi_{2}
on the right-hand side of the sequent, and use this decomposition to compute suitable formulas
\phi_{1}
and
\phi_{2}
.
We first notice that, since
{\cal S}
is not an axiom,
\phi
must be heap-satisfiable.
Furthermore, since
{\cal S}
is valid, it cannot be an anti-axiom by Lemma
4.38
, thus

\mathit{alloc}(\psi_{i})\subseteq\mathit{alloc}(\phi)
for
i=1,2
. Since
\psi_{i}\not=\mathit{emp}
, this entails that
\mathit{alloc}(\phi)
is not empty.
Let
U_{1},U_{2}
be disjoint infinite subsets of
\mathfrak{U}_{\mathtt{loc}}
and let
\mathfrak{s}
be an injective store such that

\mathfrak{s}(\mathcal{V})\cap U_{i}=\emptyset
, for
i=1,2
.
Note that such
U_{1},U_{2}
and
\mathfrak{s}
exist since
\mathfrak{U}_{{\mathtt{s}}}
is infinite, for all
{\mathtt{s}}\in\mathfrak{S}
.
By Lemma
4.36
applied with
U=U_{1}
and with any variable
x\in\mathit{alloc}(\phi)
, there exists an
\mathfrak{R}
-model
(\mathfrak{s},\mathfrak{h})
of
\phi
such that

\mathit{dom}(\mathfrak{h})\subseteq U_{1}\cup\mathfrak{s}(\mathit{alloc}(\phi))
.
Since
\mathfrak{s}
is injective and
\xi
is a conjunction of disequations, necessarily
\mathfrak{s}\models\xi
(observe that
\xi
contains no disequation of the form
u\not\approx u
as otherwise
{\cal S}
would be an axiom).
If
there exists
x\in V
such that
\mathfrak{s}(x)\in\mathit{dom}(\mathfrak{h})
, then since
\mathit{dom}(\mathfrak{h})\subseteq U_{1}\cup\mathfrak{s}(\mathit{alloc}(\phi))
and
\mathfrak{s}(\mathcal{V})\cap U_{1}=\emptyset
,
there must exist
x^{\prime}\in\mathit{alloc}(\phi)
such that

\mathfrak{s}(x)=\mathfrak{s}(x^{\prime})
.
Since
\mathfrak{s}
is injective, necessarily

x=x^{\prime}
, which entails that
{\cal S}
is an axiom, contradicting the hypotheses of the lemma.
We deduce that
\mathfrak{s}(V)\cap\mathit{dom}(\mathfrak{h})=\emptyset
.
Since
\mathfrak{s}
is injective, it is injective on
V
, as otherwise
V
would contain two occurrences of the same variable and
{\cal S}
would be an axiom.
Because
{\cal S}
is valid,
we deduce that
(\mathfrak{s},\mathfrak{h})\models_{\mathfrak{R}}(\psi_{1}*\psi_{2})
, hence there exist disjoint heaps
\mathfrak{h}_{1}
and
\mathfrak{h}_{2}
such that
\mathfrak{h}=\mathfrak{h}_{1}\uplus\mathfrak{h}_{2}
and

(\mathfrak{s},\mathfrak{h}_{i})\models_{\mathfrak{R}}\psi_{i}
, for
i=1,2
.
Let
\phi_{i}
be the separating conjunction of all the spatial atoms

\alpha
occurring in
\phi
such that
\mathfrak{s}(\mathit{alloc}(\alpha))\subseteq\mathit{dom}(\mathfrak{h}_{i})
.
By Lemma
4.2
,

\mathfrak{s}(\mathit{alloc}(\phi))\subseteq\mathit{dom}(\mathfrak{h})=\mathit{dom}(\mathfrak{h}_{1})\cup\mathit{dom}(\mathfrak{h}_{2})
, hence every atom
\alpha

occurs in either
\phi_{1}
or
\phi_{2}
, and since
\mathfrak{h}_{1}

and
\mathfrak{h}_{2}
are disjoint, no atom
\alpha
can occur both in
\phi_{1}
and
\phi_{2}
.
Therefore,
\phi=\phi_{1}*\phi_{2}
.

We now prove that
(\mathfrak{s},\mathfrak{h}_{i})\models_{\mathfrak{R}}\phi_{i}
for
i=1,2
. To this aim we use Lemma
5.28
, thus we verify that all the hypotheses of the lemma are satisfied. Since
\mathit{dom}(\mathfrak{h})\subseteq U_{1}\cup\mathfrak{s}(\mathit{alloc}(\phi))
and
U_{1}\cap\mathfrak{s}(\mathcal{V})=\emptyset
, we have
\mathit{dom}(\mathfrak{h})\cap\mathfrak{s}(\mathcal{V})\subseteq\mathfrak{s}(\mathit{alloc}(\phi))
, i.e.
(\mathit{dom}(\mathfrak{h}_{1})\cup\mathit{dom}(\mathfrak{h}_{2}))\cap\mathfrak{s}(\mathcal{V})=\mathfrak{s}(\mathit{alloc}(\phi_{1}*\phi_{2}))
.
Furthermore,
\mathfrak{s}(\mathit{alloc}(\phi_{i}))\subseteq\mathit{dom}(\mathfrak{h}_{i})
for
i=1,2
by definition of
\phi_{i}
,
(\mathfrak{s},\mathfrak{h}_{1}\uplus\mathfrak{h}_{2})=(\mathfrak{s},\mathfrak{h})\models_{\mathfrak{R}}\phi=\phi_{1}*\phi_{2}
,
(\mathfrak{s},\mathfrak{h}_{i})\models_{\mathfrak{R}}\psi_{i}
(for all
i=1,2
) and
\mathit{alloc}(\psi_{1}*\psi_{2})\subseteq\mathit{alloc}(\phi_{1}*\phi_{2})
.
By Lemma
5.28
,
we deduce that
(\mathfrak{s},\mathfrak{h}_{i})\models_{\mathfrak{R}}\phi_{i}
for
i=1,2
.

It is clear that there is an application of

S
on
{\cal S}=(\phi_{1}*\phi_{2})\curlywedge\xi\models_{\mathfrak{R}}(\psi_{1}*\psi_{2})\curlywedge\top
yielding
the premises
\phi_{i}\curlywedge\xi\vdash_{\mathfrak{R}}^{V_{i}}\psi_{i}
for
i=1,2
, where
V_{i}=V\cup\mathit{alloc}(\phi_{3-i})
. There remains to prove that these premises are valid and that the application of the rule is allowed by the strategy.
Assume that one of these premises, say
\phi_{1}\curlywedge\xi\vdash_{\mathfrak{R}}^{V_{1}}\psi_{1}
, is not valid.
By Corollary
5.26
applied with
U=U_{2}
,
\phi_{1}\curlywedge\xi\vdash_{\mathfrak{R}}^{V_{1}}\psi_{1}
admits a counter-model

(\mathfrak{s}^{\prime},\mathfrak{h}_{1}^{\prime})
, where
\mathit{dom}(\mathfrak{h}_{1}^{\prime})\cup\mathfrak{s}^{\prime}(\mathcal{V}_{\mathtt{loc}})\subseteq U_{2}
.
By construction we have

\mathfrak{s}^{\prime}(V_{1})\cap\mathit{dom}(\mathfrak{h}_{1}^{\prime})=\emptyset
, thus

\mathfrak{s}^{\prime}(V)\cap\mathit{dom}(\mathfrak{h}_{1}^{\prime})=\emptyset
since
V\subseteq V_{1}
.
Moreover,
\mathfrak{s}^{\prime}
is injective on
V_{1}=V\cup\mathit{alloc}(\phi_{2})
.
By Lemma
4.36
,
\phi_{2}
therefore admits a model
(\mathfrak{s}^{\prime},\mathfrak{h}_{2}^{\prime})
with

\mathit{dom}(\mathfrak{h}_{2}^{\prime})\subseteq U_{1}\cup\mathfrak{s}^{\prime}(\mathit{alloc}(\phi_{2}))
.
We show that
\mathfrak{h}_{1}^{\prime}
and
\mathfrak{h}_{2}^{\prime}
are disjoint.
Assume for the sake of contradiction that
\ell\in\mathit{dom}(\mathfrak{h}_{1}^{\prime})\cap\mathit{dom}(\mathfrak{h}_{2}^{\prime})
. Since
U_{1}\cap U_{2}=\emptyset
,
we have

\mathit{dom}(\mathfrak{h}_{1}^{\prime})\cap\mathit{dom}(\mathfrak{h}_{2}^{\prime})\subseteq\mathfrak{s}^{\prime}(\mathit{alloc}(\phi_{2}))
,
thus

\ell\in\mathfrak{s}^{\prime}(\mathit{alloc}(\phi_{2}))
.
But since
\mathfrak{s}^{\prime}(V_{1})\cap\mathit{dom}(\mathfrak{h}_{1}^{\prime})=\emptyset
and
\mathit{alloc}(\phi_{2})\subseteq V_{1}

we also have
\ell\not\in\mathfrak{s}^{\prime}(\mathit{alloc}(\phi_{2}))
, a contradiction.
Thus
\mathfrak{h}_{1}^{\prime}
and
\mathfrak{h}_{2}^{\prime}
are disjoint
and we have
(\mathfrak{s}^{\prime},\mathfrak{h}_{1}^{\prime}\uplus\mathfrak{h}_{2}^{\prime})\models_{\mathfrak{R}}(\phi_{1}*\phi_{2})\curlywedge\xi=\phi\curlywedge\xi
.
If
\mathfrak{s}^{\prime}(V)\cap\mathit{dom}(\mathfrak{h}_{2}^{\prime})
contains a location
\ell
then since
U_{1}\cap U_{2}=\emptyset
, necessarily
\ell=\mathfrak{s}^{\prime}(x)
, for some
x\in\mathit{alloc}(\phi_{2})
, and since
\mathfrak{s}^{\prime}
is injective on
V_{1}=V\cup\mathit{alloc}(\phi_{2})
and
\ell\in\mathfrak{s}^{\prime}(V)
, necessarily,
x\in V
and
{\cal S}
is an axiom, which contradicts the hypotheses of the lemma.
We deduce that
\mathfrak{s}^{\prime}(V)\cap\mathit{dom}(\mathfrak{h}_{2}^{\prime})=\emptyset
, and
since
\mathfrak{s}^{\prime}(V)\cap\mathit{dom}(\mathfrak{h}_{1}^{\prime})=\emptyset
, that

\mathfrak{s}^{\prime}(V)\cap\mathit{dom}(\mathfrak{h}_{1}^{\prime}\uplus\mathfrak{h}_{2}^{\prime})=\emptyset
.
Furthermore, by construction,
\mathfrak{s}^{\prime}
is injective on
V
.
Since

{\cal S}
is valid, this entails that
(\mathfrak{s}^{\prime},\mathfrak{h}_{1}^{\prime}\uplus\mathfrak{h}_{2}^{\prime})\models_{\mathfrak{R}}(\psi_{1}*\psi_{2})
, hence

\mathfrak{h}_{1}^{\prime}\uplus\mathfrak{h}_{2}^{\prime}=\mathfrak{h}_{1}^{\prime\prime}\uplus\mathfrak{h}_{2}^{\prime\prime}
, where
(\mathfrak{s}^{\prime},\mathfrak{h}_{i}^{\prime\prime})\models_{\mathfrak{R}}\psi_{i}
.
By Lemma
3.9
, since

(\mathfrak{s}^{\prime},\mathfrak{h}_{2}^{\prime})\models_{\mathfrak{R}}\psi_{2}
and
\mathfrak{R}
is deterministic, we get

\mathfrak{h}_{2}^{\prime\prime}=\mathfrak{h}_{2}^{\prime}
, and therefore
\mathfrak{h}_{1}^{\prime\prime}=\mathfrak{h}_{1}^{\prime}
, thus
(\mathfrak{s}^{\prime},\mathfrak{h}_{1}^{\prime})\models_{\mathfrak{R}}\psi_{1}
.
This contradicts the fact that
(\mathfrak{s}^{\prime},\mathfrak{h}_{1}^{\prime})
is a counter-model of

\phi_{1}\curlywedge\xi\vdash_{\mathfrak{R}}^{V_{2}}\psi_{1}
.

If
{\cal S}
is narrow, then the proof is completed, since
all the applications of rule

S
are considered by the strategy in this case.
If
{\cal S}
is not narrow, then the application of the rule may be blocked by Condition
7
of Definition
4.43
. In this case, this means that there exist
\phi_{1}^{\prime},\phi_{2}^{\prime}
such that
\phi=\phi_{1}^{\prime}*\phi_{2}^{\prime}

and
\phi_{1}^{\prime}\curlywedge\xi\vdash_{\mathfrak{R}}^{V_{1}^{\prime}}\psi_{1}
is valid, where

V_{1}^{\prime}=V\cup\mathit{alloc}(\phi_{2}^{\prime})
.
We assume that the other premise,

\phi_{2}^{\prime}\curlywedge\xi\vdash_{\mathfrak{R}}^{V_{2}^{\prime}}\psi_{2}
(with
V_{2}^{\prime}=V\cup\mathit{alloc}(\phi_{1}^{\prime})
)
is not valid, and we derive a contradiction.
By Corollary
5.26
, this premise admits
a counter-model

(\mathfrak{s}^{\prime\prime},\mathfrak{h}^{\prime\prime}_{2})
such that
\mathit{dom}(\mathfrak{h}^{\prime\prime}_{2})\cup\mathfrak{s}^{\prime\prime}(\mathcal{V}_{\mathtt{loc}})\subseteq U_{1}
.
We have
(\mathfrak{s}^{\prime\prime},\mathfrak{h}_{2}^{\prime\prime})\models_{\mathfrak{R}}\phi_{2}^{\prime}
,
\mathfrak{s}^{\prime\prime}\models\xi
,

\mathfrak{s}^{\prime\prime}(V_{2}^{\prime})\cap\mathit{dom}(\mathfrak{h}_{2}^{\prime\prime})=\emptyset
,

\mathfrak{s}^{\prime\prime}
is injective on
V_{2}^{\prime}

and
(\mathfrak{s},\mathfrak{h}_{2}^{\prime\prime})\not\models_{\mathfrak{R}}\psi_{2}
.
By Lemma
4.36
,

\phi_{1}^{\prime}
admits a model

(\mathfrak{s}^{\prime\prime},\mathfrak{h}_{1}^{\prime\prime})
such that

\mathit{dom}(\mathfrak{h}_{1}^{\prime\prime})\subseteq U_{2}\cup\mathfrak{s}^{\prime\prime}(\mathit{alloc}(\phi_{1}^{\prime}))
.

Note that

\mathfrak{h}_{1}^{\prime\prime}
and
\mathfrak{h}_{2}^{\prime\prime}
are disjoint: if
\ell\in\mathit{dom}(\mathfrak{h}_{1}^{\prime\prime})\cap\mathit{dom}(\mathfrak{h}_{2}^{\prime\prime})
then, as

{\mathit{dom}(\mathfrak{h}_{1}^{\prime\prime})\subseteq U_{2}\cup\mathfrak{s}^{\prime\prime}(\mathit{alloc}(\phi_{1}^{\prime}))}
and
\mathit{dom}(\mathfrak{h}^{\prime\prime}_{2})\cup\mathfrak{s}^{\prime\prime}(\mathcal{V}_{\mathtt{loc}})\subseteq U_{1}
, with
U_{1}\cap U_{2}=\emptyset
, we get
\ell\in\mathfrak{s}^{\prime\prime}(\mathit{alloc}(\phi_{1}^{\prime}))
, so that

\ell\in\mathfrak{s}^{\prime\prime}(V_{2}^{\prime})
, contradicting the fact that
\mathfrak{s}^{\prime\prime}(V_{2}^{\prime})\cap\mathit{dom}(\mathfrak{h}_{2}^{\prime\prime})=\emptyset
.

We prove that
\mathfrak{s}^{\prime\prime}
is injective on
V_{1}^{\prime}
, and that
\mathit{dom}(\mathfrak{h}_{1}^{\prime\prime})\cap\mathfrak{s}^{\prime\prime}(V_{1})=\emptyset
, which entails that

(\mathfrak{s}^{\prime\prime},\mathfrak{h}_{1}^{\prime\prime})\models_{\mathfrak{R}}\psi_{1}
, since
\phi_{1}^{\prime}\curlywedge\xi\vdash_{\mathfrak{R}}^{V_{1}^{\prime}}\psi_{1}
is valid.
If there exist
x_{1},x_{2}
such that
\{x_{1},x_{2}\}\subseteq_{m}V_{1}^{\prime}=V\cup\mathit{alloc}(\phi_{2}^{\prime})
and
\mathfrak{s}^{\prime\prime}(x_{1})=\mathfrak{s}^{\prime\prime}(x_{2})
then, as

\mathfrak{s}^{\prime\prime}
is injective on
V_{2}^{\prime}=V\cup\mathit{alloc}(\phi_{1}^{\prime})
, necessarily at least one of the variables
x_{1},x_{2}
– say
x_{1}
–
is in
\mathit{alloc}(\phi_{2}^{\prime})
. As
(\mathfrak{s}^{\prime\prime},\mathfrak{h}_{2}^{\prime\prime})\models\phi_{2}^{\prime}
, we get

\mathfrak{s}^{\prime\prime}(x_{1})\in\mathit{dom}(\mathfrak{h}_{2}^{\prime\prime})
(by Lemma
4.2
), and

\{x_{1},x_{2}\}\not\subseteq_{m}\mathit{alloc}(\phi_{2}^{\prime})
(by Corollary
4.4
).
This entails that

\mathfrak{s}^{\prime\prime}(x_{1})\not\in\mathfrak{s}^{\prime\prime}(V_{2}^{\prime})
(as
\mathfrak{s}^{\prime\prime}(V_{2}^{\prime})\cap\mathit{dom}(\mathfrak{h}_{2}^{\prime\prime})=\emptyset
) and
x_{2}\in V\subseteq V_{2}^{\prime}
, yielding a contradiction, since
\mathfrak{s}^{\prime\prime}(x_{1})=\mathfrak{s}^{\prime\prime}(x_{2})
.
Now assume that there exists
y\in V_{1}^{\prime}=V\cup\mathit{alloc}(\phi_{2}^{\prime})
such that
\mathfrak{s}^{\prime\prime}(y)\in\mathit{dom}(\mathfrak{h}_{1}^{\prime\prime})
. As
\mathit{dom}(\mathfrak{h}_{1}^{\prime\prime})\subseteq U_{2}\cup\mathfrak{s}^{\prime\prime}(\mathit{alloc}(\phi_{1}^{\prime}))
and
\mathit{dom}(\mathfrak{h}^{\prime\prime}_{2})\cup\mathfrak{s}^{\prime\prime}(\mathcal{V}_{\mathtt{loc}})\subseteq U_{1}
, we get

\mathfrak{s}^{\prime\prime}(y)\in\mathfrak{s}^{\prime\prime}(\mathit{alloc}(\phi_{1}^{\prime}))
, i.e., there exists
y^{\prime}\in\mathit{alloc}(\phi_{1}^{\prime})
such that

\mathfrak{s}^{\prime\prime}(y)=\mathfrak{s}^{\prime\prime}(y^{\prime})
. If
y\in V
, then
\{y,y^{\prime}\}\subseteq_{m}V_{2}^{\prime}
, contradicting the fact that
\mathfrak{s}^{\prime\prime}
is injective on
V_{2}^{\prime}
.
Thus
y\in\mathit{alloc}(\phi_{2}^{\prime})
, so that
\mathfrak{s}(y)\in\mathit{dom}(\mathfrak{h}_{2}^{\prime\prime})
(by Lemma
4.2
), contradicting the fact that
\mathfrak{h}_{1}^{\prime\prime}
and
\mathfrak{h}_{2}^{\prime\prime}
are disjoint.

Consequently,

(\mathfrak{s}^{\prime\prime},\mathfrak{h}_{1}^{\prime\prime}\uplus\mathfrak{h}_{2}^{\prime\prime})\models_{\mathfrak{R}}\phi_{1}^{\prime}*\phi_{2}^{\prime}=\phi
.
As
\mathfrak{s}^{\prime\prime}\models\xi
and
{\mathfrak{s}^{\prime\prime}(V)\cap\mathit{dom}(\mathfrak{h}_{i}^{\prime\prime})\subseteq\mathfrak{s}(V_{i})\cap\mathit{dom}(\mathfrak{h}_{i}^{\prime\prime})}
is empty,
\mathfrak{s}^{\prime\prime}
is injective on
V
, and
{\cal S}
is valid,
we get
(\mathfrak{s}^{\prime\prime},\mathfrak{h}_{1}^{\prime\prime}\uplus\mathfrak{h}_{2}^{\prime\prime})\models_{\mathfrak{R}}\psi_{1}*\psi_{2}
.
Therefore, there exist disjoint heaps
\widehat{\mathfrak{h}}^{\prime\prime}_{i}
(for
i=1,2
), such that

(\mathfrak{s}^{\prime\prime},\widehat{\mathfrak{h}}_{i}^{\prime\prime})\models_{\mathfrak{R}}\psi_{i}
and

\mathfrak{h}_{1}^{\prime\prime}\uplus\mathfrak{h}_{2}^{\prime\prime}=\widehat{\mathfrak{h}}_{1}^{\prime\prime}\uplus\widehat{\mathfrak{h}}^{\prime\prime}_{2}
.
By Lemma
3.9
, since
(\mathfrak{s}^{\prime\prime},\mathfrak{h}_{1}^{\prime\prime})\models_{\mathfrak{R}}\psi_{1}
, we deduce that
\mathfrak{h}_{1}^{\prime\prime}=\widehat{\mathfrak{h}}_{1}^{\prime\prime}
, thus
\mathfrak{h}_{2}^{\prime\prime}=\widehat{\mathfrak{h}}_{2}^{\prime\prime}
, hence
and
(\mathfrak{s}^{\prime\prime},\mathfrak{h}_{2}^{\prime\prime})\models_{\mathfrak{R}}\psi_{2}
,
which contradicts the fact that
(\mathfrak{s}^{\prime\prime},\mathfrak{h}_{2}^{\prime\prime})
is a counter-model of
\phi_{2}^{\prime}\curlywedge\xi\vdash_{\mathfrak{R}}^{V_{2}^{\prime}}\psi_{2}
.

We handle the case of the other rules, more precisely we show that there is always an inference rule that applies on a valid sequent (if it is not an axiom):

Lemma 5.34

For any valid sequent
\phi\curlywedge\xi\vdash_{\mathfrak{R}}^{V}\psi\curlywedge\zeta
that is not an axiom, there exists
an application of the inference rules that yields valid premises.

Proof 5.35

Since
\phi\curlywedge\xi\vdash_{\mathfrak{R}}^{V}\psi\curlywedge\zeta
is not an axiom,
\phi
is heap-satisfiable,
V\cap\mathit{alloc}(\phi)=\emptyset
and
V
contains at most one occurrence of each variable.
Note that if an invertible rule applies on
\phi\curlywedge\xi\vdash_{\mathfrak{R}}^{V}\psi\curlywedge\zeta

then the result holds, since the premises are necessarily valid. Thus we may assume that

\phi\curlywedge\xi\vdash_{\mathfrak{R}}^{V}\psi\curlywedge\zeta
is irreducible by all invertible rules, hence
(by Lemma
4.26
) by all
the rules except

S
.

Let
\mathfrak{s}
be an injective store such that the set

U=\mathfrak{U}_{\mathtt{loc}}\setminus\mathfrak{s}(\mathcal{V})
is infinite.
If
\phi\not=\mathit{emp}
, then
\phi
contains at least one variable of sort
\mathtt{loc}
, and by Lemma
4.36
, we deduce that

\phi
admits a model
(\mathfrak{s},\mathfrak{h})
such that

\mathit{dom}(\mathfrak{h})\subseteq U\cup\mathfrak{s}(\mathit{alloc}(\phi))
.
If
\phi=\mathit{emp}
then the same property trivially holds, with
\mathfrak{h}=\emptyset
.
Note in particular that the assertion
\mathit{dom}(\mathfrak{h})\subseteq U\cup\mathfrak{s}(\mathit{alloc}(\phi))
entails that

\mathfrak{s}(V)\cap\mathit{dom}(\mathfrak{h})=\emptyset
since
\mathfrak{s}(V)\cap U=\emptyset
,
V\cap\mathit{alloc}(\phi)=\emptyset
and
\mathfrak{s}
is injective.

If
\xi
contains an equation then rule

R
applies, which contradicts our assumption that the sequent is irreducible by all invertible rules; thus
\xi
is a conjunction of disequations. Furthermore, none of these disequations may be of the form
x\not\approx x
because otherwise
\phi\curlywedge\xi\vdash_{\mathfrak{R}}^{V}\psi\curlywedge\zeta
would be an axiom.
This entails that
\mathfrak{s}\models\xi
. Since
\phi\curlywedge\xi\vdash_{\mathfrak{R}}^{V}\psi\curlywedge\zeta
is valid,
\mathfrak{s}(V)\cap\mathit{dom}(\mathfrak{h})=\emptyset
and
\mathfrak{s}
is injective (hence injective on
V
because
V
contains at most one occurrence of each variable), we deduce that

(\mathfrak{s},\mathfrak{h})\models_{\mathfrak{R}}\psi\curlywedge\zeta.\quad\quad(\dagger)

Assume that
\zeta
contains an equation
x\approx y
. If
x=y
then Rule

E
applies, and
otherwise, we necessarily have
\mathfrak{s}(x)\not=\mathfrak{s}(y)
, hence
(\mathfrak{s},\mathfrak{h})\not\models_{\mathfrak{R}}\psi\curlywedge\zeta

which contradicts (
\dagger
).
Thus
\zeta
contains no equation.
Now assume that
\zeta
contains a disequation
x\not\approx y
.
If
\{x,y\}\subseteq\mathcal{C}
(i.e., both
x
and
y
are constants) or
\{x,y\}\subseteq_{m}\mathit{alloc}(\phi)\cup V
then
\phi\curlywedge\xi\triangleright_{V}x\not\approx y
, and

E
applies, which is impossible by hypothesis.
If
x=y
then
x\not\approx y
is unsatisfiable, which contradicts (
\dagger
).
Let
\mathfrak{s}^{\prime}
be a store verifying
\mathfrak{s}^{\prime}(x)=\mathfrak{s}^{\prime}(y)
, that maps all other variables to pairwise distinct
elements of the appropriate sort, also distinct from
\mathfrak{s}(x)
, and such that
U^{\prime}=\mathfrak{U}_{\mathtt{loc}}\setminus\mathfrak{s}^{\prime}(\mathcal{V})
is infinite. Such a store exists since
\mathfrak{U}_{\mathtt{loc}}
is infinite and
\{x,y\}\not\subseteq\mathcal{C}
. If
\mathfrak{s}^{\prime}\not\models\xi
,
then since
\xi
contains no equation,
x\not\approx y
must occur in
\xi
.
Then the rule

E
applies, which contradicts our assumption that the sequent is irreducible by all invertible rules.
Thus
\mathfrak{s}^{\prime}\models\xi
.
Since
\{x,y\}\not\subseteq_{m}\mathit{alloc}(\phi)
,
\mathfrak{s}^{\prime}
is injective on
\mathit{alloc}(\phi)
.
By Lemma
4.36
, we deduce that

\phi
admits a model of the form
(\mathfrak{s}^{\prime},\mathfrak{h}^{\prime})
with
\mathit{dom}(\mathfrak{h}^{\prime})\subseteq U^{\prime}\cup\mathfrak{s}^{\prime}(\mathit{alloc}(\phi))
. If
\mathfrak{s}^{\prime}(V)\cap\mathit{dom}(\mathfrak{h}^{\prime})
contains a location
\ell
, then

\ell=\mathfrak{s}^{\prime}(z)=\mathfrak{s}^{\prime}(z^{\prime})
, for some
z\in\mathit{alloc}(\phi)
and
z^{\prime}\in V
.
Since
\mathit{alloc}(\phi)\cap V=\emptyset
and
\mathfrak{s}^{\prime}
is injective on all pair of variables except on
\{x,y\}
, necessarily we have either
x=z
and
y=z^{\prime}
, or
x=z^{\prime}
and
y=z
, which means that
\{x,y\}\subseteq_{m}\mathit{alloc}(\phi)\cup V
, hence

E
applies, contradicting our assumption that the sequent is irreducible by all invertible rules.
We deduce that

\mathfrak{s}^{\prime}(V)\cap\mathit{dom}(\mathfrak{h}^{\prime})=\emptyset
.
Since
\{x,y\}\not\subseteq_{m}V
,
\mathfrak{s}^{\prime}
is necessarily injective on
V
.
As
\phi\curlywedge\xi\vdash_{\mathfrak{R}}^{V}\psi\curlywedge\zeta
is valid, we deduce that

(\mathfrak{s}^{\prime},\mathfrak{h}^{\prime})\models_{\mathfrak{R}}\psi\curlywedge\zeta

hence
\mathfrak{s}^{\prime}(x)\not=\mathfrak{s}^{\prime}(y)
, which contradicts the definition of
\mathfrak{s}^{\prime}
.
Therefore,
\zeta=\top
.

If
\psi=\mathit{emp}
then necessarily
\phi=\mathit{emp}
, since otherwise by Corollary
4.6
we would have

\mathfrak{h}\not=\emptyset
, which contradicts
(\dagger)
. Hence in this case,
\phi\curlywedge\xi\vdash_{\mathfrak{R}}^{V}\psi\curlywedge\zeta
is of the form
\mathit{emp}\curlywedge\xi\vdash_{\mathfrak{R}}^{V}\mathit{emp}
and is an axiom.

If
\psi
is of the form
\psi_{1}*\psi_{2}
, where
\psi_{i}\not=\mathit{emp}
for
i=1,2
, then the proof follows from Lemma
5.32
.
Thus, we may assume that
\psi
is a spatial atom. Let
x
be the root of
\psi
.
If
x\not\in\mathit{roots}(\phi)
, then we have

(\mathfrak{s},\mathfrak{h})\not\models_{\mathfrak{R}}\psi
by Lemma
4.2
, which contradicts (
\dagger
).
Therefore,
\phi
contains a spatial atom with root
x
.
If this spatial atom is a predicate
atom then

U
can be applied.
Now assume that
\phi
contains a points-to atom
x\mapsto(y_{1},\dots,y_{n})
, i.e., is of the form

x\mapsto(y_{1},\dots,y_{n})*\phi^{\prime}
(modulo AC).
Since
\psi
is a spatial atom with root
x
, there are two cases to consider:

1.

\psi
is of the form
x\mapsto(y_{1}^{\prime},\dots,y_{n}^{\prime})
.
We have
\mathfrak{h}=\{(\mathfrak{s}(x),\mathfrak{s}(y_{1}),\dots,\mathfrak{s}(y_{n}))\}\uplus\mathfrak{h}^{\prime}
, where
\mathfrak{h}^{\prime}
is a heap such that
(\mathfrak{s},\mathfrak{h}^{\prime})\models_{\mathfrak{R}}\phi^{\prime}
and
\mathfrak{s}(x)\not\in\mathit{dom}(\mathfrak{h}^{\prime})
.
By
(\dagger)
, we have
(\mathfrak{s},\mathfrak{h})\models_{\mathfrak{R}}\psi
, thus

\mathfrak{h}=\{(\mathfrak{s}(x),\mathfrak{s}(y_{1}^{\prime}),\dots,\mathfrak{s}(y_{n}^{\prime}))\}
. This entails that

\mathfrak{h}^{\prime}=\emptyset
(hence

\phi^{\prime}=\mathit{emp}
by Corollary
4.6
), and that
\mathfrak{s}(y_{i})=\mathfrak{s}(y_{i}^{\prime})
for all
i=1,\dots,n
. Since
\mathfrak{s}

is injective, we have
y_{i}=y_{i}^{\prime}
for all
i=1,\dots,n
.
Therefore,
\psi=\phi
, and
\phi\curlywedge\xi\vdash_{\mathfrak{R}}^{V}\psi\curlywedge\zeta
is an axiom (because
\zeta=\top
), which contradicts the hypothesis of the lemma.

2.

\psi
is a predicate atom.
By (
\dagger
), we have

(\mathfrak{s},\mathfrak{h})\models_{\mathfrak{R}}\psi
and by definition there exists a formula
\gamma^{\prime}
such that

\psi\Leftarrow_{\mathfrak{R}}\gamma^{\prime}
,
where
(\mathfrak{s}^{\prime},\mathfrak{h})\models_{\mathfrak{R}}\gamma^{\prime}
for some associate
\mathfrak{s}^{\prime}
of
\mathfrak{s}
w.r.t.
\mathcal{V}(\gamma^{\prime})\setminus\mathcal{V}(\psi)
, and in particular we have
\mathfrak{s}(x)=\mathfrak{s}^{\prime}(x)
.
As the rules in
\mathfrak{R}
are
\mathtt{P}
-rules,

\gamma^{\prime}
is of the form
{(x\mapsto(u_{1},\dots,u_{m})*\psi^{\prime})\curlywedge\zeta^{\prime}}
, with
\mathcal{V}(\gamma^{\prime})\subseteq\mathcal{V}(\psi)\cup\{u_{1},\dots,u_{m}\}
,
and we have
\mathfrak{h}(\mathfrak{s}(x))=\mathfrak{h}(\mathfrak{s}^{\prime}(x))=(\mathfrak{s}^{\prime}(u_{1}),\dots,\mathfrak{s}^{\prime}(u_{m}))
.
Since
\phi
contains a points-to atom
x\mapsto(y_{1},\dots,y_{n})
and
(\mathfrak{s},\mathfrak{h})\models_{\mathfrak{R}}\phi
, necessarily

\mathfrak{h}(\mathfrak{s}(x))=(\mathfrak{s}(y_{1}),\dots,\mathfrak{s}(y_{n}))
. Consequently
n=m
and
\mathfrak{s}(y_{i})=\mathfrak{s}^{\prime}(u_{i})
for all
i=1,\dots,n
.
Let
\sigma
be the substitution mapping every variable
u_{i}
not occurring in
\mathcal{V}(\psi)
to

y_{i}
. Note that
\sigma
is well-defined: if
u_{i}=u_{j}
then
\mathfrak{s}(y_{i})=\mathfrak{s}(y_{j})
and
y_{i}=y_{j}
since
\mathfrak{s}
is injective.
It is straightforward to check that
\mathfrak{s}^{\prime}(z)=\mathfrak{s}(z\sigma)
for all variables
z\in\mathcal{V}
, using the definition of
\sigma
and the fact that
\mathfrak{s}^{\prime}
and
\mathfrak{s}
coincide on all variables not occurring in
u_{1},\dots,u_{n}
.
If

I
applies with this substitution
\sigma
then the proof is completed. Otherwise,
we must have
\phi\curlywedge\xi{\not\triangleright_{V}}\zeta^{\prime}\sigma
, i.e.,
\zeta^{\prime}
must contain a disequation
u^{\prime}\not\approx v^{\prime}
such that

\phi\curlywedge\xi{\not\triangleright_{V}}u^{\prime}\sigma\not\approx v^{\prime}\sigma
. Note that this entails that
u^{\prime}\sigma
and
v^{\prime}\sigma
cannot both be constants.
Consider a store
\mathfrak{s}^{\prime\prime}
verifying
\mathfrak{s}^{\prime\prime}(u^{\prime}\sigma)=\mathfrak{s}^{\prime\prime}(v^{\prime}\sigma)
, and mapping all other variables to pairwise distinct elements of the appropriate sort. We may assume that
\mathfrak{s}^{\prime\prime}
is chosen in such a way that the set
U^{\prime}=\mathfrak{U}\setminus\mathfrak{s}^{\prime\prime}(\mathcal{V})
is infinite.
By definition,
\mathfrak{s}^{\prime\prime}\not\models\zeta^{\prime}\sigma
, and since
\phi\curlywedge\xi{\not\triangleright_{V}}u^{\prime}\sigma\not\approx v^{\prime}\sigma
, we have
\{u^{\prime}\sigma,v^{\prime}\sigma\}\not\subseteq_{m}V\cup\mathit{alloc}(\phi)
, thus

\mathfrak{s}^{\prime\prime}

is injective on
\mathit{alloc}(\phi)\cup V
and
\mathfrak{s}^{\prime\prime}\models\xi
(since
\xi
is a conjunction of disequations not containing
u^{\prime}\sigma\not\approx v^{\prime}\sigma
).
By Lemma
4.36
,
\phi
admits a model
(\mathfrak{s}^{\prime\prime},\mathfrak{h}^{\prime\prime})
such that
\mathit{dom}(\mathfrak{h}^{\prime\prime})\subseteq U^{\prime\prime}\cup\mathfrak{s}^{\prime\prime}(\mathit{alloc}(\phi))
. This entails that
\mathfrak{s}^{\prime\prime}(V)\cap\mathit{dom}(\mathfrak{h}^{\prime\prime})=\emptyset
.
Since
\mathfrak{s}^{\prime\prime}
is injective on
V
and
\phi\curlywedge\xi\vdash_{\mathfrak{R}}^{V}\psi\curlywedge\zeta
is valid, we deduce that

(\mathfrak{s}^{\prime\prime},\mathfrak{h}^{\prime\prime})\models_{\mathfrak{R}}\psi
, thus

\psi\Leftarrow_{\mathfrak{R}}\gamma^{\prime\prime}
,
where
(\hat{\mathfrak{s}},\mathfrak{h}^{\prime\prime})\models_{\mathfrak{R}}\gamma^{\prime\prime}
, for some formula
\gamma^{\prime\prime}
and associate
\hat{\mathfrak{s}}
of
\mathfrak{s}^{\prime\prime}
w.r.t.
\mathcal{V}(\gamma^{\prime\prime})\setminus\mathcal{V}(\psi)
.
Since the rules in
\mathfrak{R}
are
\mathtt{P}
-rules,

\gamma^{\prime\prime}
is of the form
(x\mapsto(u_{1}^{\prime},\dots,u_{k}^{\prime})*\psi^{\prime\prime})\curlywedge\zeta^{\prime\prime}
,
and we have
\mathfrak{h}^{\prime\prime}(\mathfrak{s}^{\prime\prime}(x))=(\hat{\mathfrak{s}}(u_{1}^{\prime}),\dots,\hat{\mathfrak{s}}(u_{k}^{\prime}))
.
We may assume, by renaming, that
\{u_{1}^{\prime},\dots,u_{k}^{\prime}\}\cap\{u_{1},\dots,u_{m}\}\subseteq\mathcal{V}(\psi)
.
Since
\phi
contains a points-to atom
x\mapsto(y_{1},\dots,y_{n})
and
(\mathfrak{s}^{\prime\prime},\mathfrak{h}^{\prime\prime})\models_{\mathfrak{R}}\phi
, necessarily

\mathfrak{h}^{\prime\prime}(\mathfrak{s}^{\prime\prime}(x))=(\mathfrak{s}^{\prime\prime}(y_{1}),\dots,\mathfrak{s}^{\prime\prime}(y_{n}))
, and thus we must have
k=n=m
and for all
i=1,\dots,n
,

\mathfrak{s}^{\prime\prime}(y_{i})=\hat{\mathfrak{s}}(u_{i}^{\prime})
.
Let
\sigma^{\prime}
be the substitution mapping every variable
u_{i}^{\prime}
not occurring in
\mathcal{V}(\psi)
to the first variable
y_{j}
in
y_{1},\dots,y_{n}
such that
\mathfrak{s}^{\prime\prime}(y_{j})=\hat{\mathfrak{s}}(u_{i}^{\prime})
, and let
\theta
be the substitution mapping every variable
y_{i}
to
y_{j}
, where
j
is the smallest index in
1,\dots,n
such that
\mathfrak{s}^{\prime\prime}\models y_{i}\approx y_{j}
.
Let
\hat{\sigma}=\sigma\cup\sigma^{\prime}
and
\theta^{\prime}=\hat{\sigma}\theta
; note that
\hat{\sigma}
is well-defined since
\mathit{dom}(\sigma)\cap\mathit{dom}(\sigma^{\prime})=\emptyset
, as
\{u_{1}^{\prime},\dots,u_{k}^{\prime}\}\cap\{u_{1},\dots,u_{m}\}\subseteq\mathcal{V}(\psi)
.
By construction,

\theta^{\prime}
is a unifier of

(u_{1},\dots,u_{n})
and

(u_{1}^{\prime},\dots,u_{n}^{\prime})
.
Since
\mathfrak{R}
is
\mathtt{loc}
-deterministic,
one of the two formulas
\zeta^{\prime}
or
\zeta^{\prime\prime}
contains a disequation

v\not\approx w
with
v\theta^{\prime}=w\theta^{\prime}
and
v,w\in\mathcal{V}_{\mathtt{loc}}
.
Note that
v\hat{\sigma}\not=w\hat{\sigma}
since otherwise

\zeta^{\prime}\sigma
or
\zeta^{\prime\prime}\sigma^{\prime}
would be unsatisfiable.
We show that
v\not\approx w
occurs in
\zeta^{\prime}
. Assume, for the sake of contradiction, that

v\not\approx w
occurs in
\zeta^{\prime\prime}
.
Then
v\hat{\sigma}=v\sigma^{\prime}
and
w\hat{\sigma}=w\sigma^{\prime}
.
By definition of
\sigma^{\prime}
and
\theta^{\prime}
, we have
z\sigma^{\prime}=z\theta^{\prime}
for all
z\in\mathit{dom}(\sigma^{\prime})
, thus we get

v\sigma^{\prime}=w\sigma^{\prime}
, hence (by definition of
\sigma^{\prime}
)
\hat{\mathfrak{s}}(v)=\hat{\mathfrak{s}}(w)
, which contradicts the fact that
(\hat{\mathfrak{s}},\mathfrak{h}^{\prime\prime})\models_{\mathfrak{R}}\gamma^{\prime\prime}=(x\mapsto(u_{1}^{\prime},\dots,u_{k}^{\prime})*\psi^{\prime\prime})\curlywedge\zeta^{\prime\prime}
.
Thus necessarily

v\not\approx w
occurs in
\zeta^{\prime}
and we have
v\hat{\sigma}=v\sigma
and
w\hat{\sigma}=w\sigma
.
As all rules are
\mathtt{P}
-rules, necessarily at least one of the two variables
v,w
(say
v
) is an existential variable, and we must have
v\in\mathit{alloc}(\psi^{\prime})
.
Since
(\mathfrak{s}^{\prime},\mathfrak{h})\models_{\mathfrak{R}}\gamma^{\prime}=(x\mapsto(u_{1},\dots,u_{m})*\psi^{\prime})\curlywedge\zeta^{\prime}
, we deduce by
Lemma
4.2
that

\mathfrak{s}^{\prime}(v)\in\mathit{dom}(\mathfrak{h})
, hence
\mathfrak{s}(v\sigma)\in\mathit{dom}(\mathfrak{h})
.
By definition of
(\mathfrak{s},\mathfrak{h})
, this entails that
\mathfrak{s}(v\sigma)\in\mathfrak{s}(\mathit{alloc}(\phi))
, and since
\mathfrak{s}
is injective we get
v\sigma\in\mathit{alloc}(\phi)
.

Assume that
w\sigma\in\mathit{alloc}(\phi)
.
Then
\mathit{alloc}(\phi)
contains spatial atoms with respective roots

w\sigma
and
v\sigma
.
These atoms must be distinct since
v\sigma\not=w\sigma
.
By Lemma
4.2
these locations are allocated in disjoint part of the heap
\mathfrak{h}^{\prime\prime}
, hence necessarily
\mathfrak{s}^{\prime\prime}(w\sigma)\not=\mathfrak{s}^{\prime\prime}(v\sigma)
.
We deduce that
v\theta^{\prime}\not=w\theta^{\prime}
, which contradicts our assumption.
Thus
w\sigma\not\in\mathit{alloc}(\phi)
, and since
\phi\curlywedge\xi\vdash_{\mathfrak{R}}^{V}\psi\curlywedge\zeta

cannot be an anti-axiom, necessarily
w\sigma\in\mathcal{V}(\psi)
.
We also have
w\sigma\not\in V
, since
\mathit{dom}(\mathfrak{h}^{\prime\prime})\cap\mathfrak{s}^{\prime\prime}(V)=\emptyset
.
We may assume that the sequent
\phi\curlywedge\xi\vdash_{\mathfrak{R}}^{V}\psi\curlywedge\zeta

is irreducible by the rule

C
, so that,
as
\psi
is a predicate atom,
\xi
contains a disequation
v\sigma\not\approx w\sigma
(since
v\sigma\in\mathit{alloc}(\phi)
,
w\sigma\in\mathcal{V}(\psi)\setminus(\mathit{alloc}(\phi)\cup V)
).
But then
we have
\mathfrak{s}^{\prime\prime}\not\models\xi
, contradicting the definition of
\mathfrak{s}^{\prime\prime}
.

The completeness result follows immediately:

Theorem 5.36

If
{\cal S}
is a valid sequent, then it admits a
fully expanded (possibly infinite) proof tree.

Proof 5.37

The theorem follows from Lemma
5.34
.

5.3
Termination and Complexity

We now show that the proof procedure runs in polynomial time. To this purpose we provide (Definition
5.38
and Lemma
5.43
) a characterization of the

I
-reducible sequents that may occur in a proof tree.

Definition 5.38

Let
\kappa\in{\mathbb{N}}
.
A sequent
{\cal S}
is an
\kappa
-companion of a symbolic heap
\phi\curlywedge\xi

if
{\cal S}
is of the form

(\phi_{1}\sigma*\phi_{2}*\phi_{3})\curlywedge(\xi_{0}\wedge\xi_{1}\wedge\xi_{2})\vdash_{\mathfrak{R}}^{V}\psi\curlywedge\top
, where:

1.

\xi_{0}
is the set of disequations occurring in
\xi
that are of the form
x\not\approx t
(up to commutativity), with
x\in(\mathcal{V}(\phi_{1}\sigma*\phi_{2}*\phi_{3})\cup\mathcal{V}(\psi))\setminus\mathcal{V}_{\mathtt{loc}}
and
t\not=x
.

2.

\xi_{1}=\bigwedge_{x\in{\cal V}^{\star}({\cal S}),y\in A}x\not\approx y
, where
A=\mathit{alloc}(\phi_{1}\sigma*\phi_{2}*\phi_{3})\setminus\mathcal{V}(\psi)
.

3.

\xi_{2}
is a conjunction of disequations of the form
u\not\approx v
, where
u,v\in\mathcal{V}(\psi)
.

4.

\psi
is a predicate atom.

5.

V\subseteq\mathcal{V}(\psi)
.

6.

Either
\phi_{1}=\mathit{emp}
, or there exists a predicate atom
\alpha
such that
\mathit{root}(\alpha)=\mathit{root}(\psi)
and

\alpha\Leftarrow_{\mathfrak{R}}\phi_{1}
.

7.

\mathit{len}(\phi_{2})\leq\kappa+1
.

8.

\sigma
is a substitution such that
\mathit{card}(\mathit{dom}(\sigma))\leq\kappa
.

9.

\phi\sigma
is of the form
\phi_{3}*\eta_{1}*\eta_{2}
(modulo AC), where

\mathit{len}(\eta_{1})\leq 2\cdot\kappa
and:

(a)

no atom
\beta
occurring in
\phi_{3}
is such that
\mathit{root}(\psi)\not\rightarrow_{\phi_{1}\sigma*\phi_{2}*\phi_{3}*\eta_{2}}^{*}\mathit{root}(\beta)
; and

(b)

no atom
\beta
occurring in
\eta_{2}
is such that
\mathit{root}(\psi)\rightarrow_{\phi_{1}\sigma*\phi_{2}*\phi_{3}*\eta_{2}}^{*}\mathit{root}(\beta)
.

Intuitively, we aim at describing sequents
{\cal S}
that are

I
-reducible and occur in some proof tree, where the formula
\phi\curlywedge\xi
is meant to denote the left-hand side of the root sequent. It is clear that the left-hand side of
{\cal S}
will contain some parts that are inherited from the formula
\phi\curlywedge\xi
, together with some other parts, that are introduced by the inference rules applied along the branch of
{\cal S}
in the proof tree.
First, observe that Conditions
4
(stating that the right-hand side of
{\cal S}
is a predicate atom
\psi
) and
5
(stating that the variables in
V
must occur in
\psi
) are easy consequences of the fact that
{\cal S}
is

I
-reducible: rules

S
and

V
cannot be applied.
The substitution
\sigma
is intended to encode the effect of the (cumulative) applications of rule

C
(in the left branch): some variables initially occurring in
\phi\curlywedge\xi
are replaced by other variables.
Note that Condition
8
bounds the size of this substitution, which is essential to avoid any exponential blow-up. As we shall see, it stems from the fact that rule

C
only applies, by Definition
4.43
(
4
), to a variable in
{\cal V}^{\star}({\cal S})
which restricts the number of applications of the rule.
The formula
\xi_{0}
denotes the set of disequations between variables of a sort distinct from
\mathtt{loc}
, which are all inherited from
\xi
(as the rules contain no disequations of this form).
On the other hand,
\xi_{1}
denotes the set of disequations
that were introduced by previous applications of

C
(in the right branch). Note that since
{\cal S}
is

I
-reducible, we may assume that all the possible applications of

C
have been applied, leading either to the elimination of a variable (encoded in
\sigma
) or to the addition of some disequation, this is why
\xi_{1}
contains the set of
all
possible disequations. The formula
\xi_{2}
denotes the set of disequations between variables in
\psi
, which are arbitrary (this is not problematic as the number of such disequations is bounded, as
\psi
is an atom).
The formula
\phi_{1}
denotes the part of the formula added in the left-hand side of the sequent by the
last
application of

U
. The formula may be empty, because such application does not always exist (for instance if
{\cal S}
is the root sequent). The atom
\alpha
then denotes the predicate atom on which

U
was previously applied. Observe that the root of this atom is always the same as that of
\psi
, by Definition
4.43
(
3
). Note also that the formula contains no atom obtained from any application of

U
occurring
before
the last one (i.e., on atoms other than
\alpha
): indeed, we shall prove that all such atoms must have been eliminated by previous decomposition steps when

I
was applied.
The formula
\phi_{1}\sigma
contains all spatial atoms inherited from the initial formula
\phi
. Note that some atoms in
\phi
may have been deleted, by previous applications of

S
.
The difficulty here is that this could lead to an exponential blow-up, since in principle one could consider all possible subsets of
\phi
.
To overcome this issue, we refine the criterion by taking into account all the additional reachability conditions that prevent
{\cal S}
from being an axiom or an anti-axiom.
We thus further split the conjunction of atoms that were deleted from
\phi
into two parts
\eta_{1}*\eta_{2}
, where
\eta_{1}
contains a
bounded
number of atoms that can be chosen in an
arbitrary
way, and
\eta_{2}
may contain an
arbitrary
number of atoms, but is fully determined by the choice of
\phi_{1},\phi_{2}
and
\eta_{1}
.

The following lemma shows that a formula has only polynomially many
\kappa
-companions, up to a renaming of variables.

Lemma 5.39

Let
\phi\curlywedge\xi
be a spatial formula and

E
be a set of variables.
For every fixed number
\kappa
,
if
\mathit{ar}_{\mathit{max}}(\mathfrak{R})\leq\kappa
(i.e., if the maximal arity of the predicate symbols in bounded by
\kappa
), then
the number of sequents
{\cal S}
that are
\kappa
-companions of
\phi\curlywedge\xi
and such that
\mathcal{V}({\cal S})\subseteq E

is polynomial w.r.t.
|\phi|+|\mathfrak{R}|+\mathit{card}(E)
(up to AC), and the size of
{\cal S}
is polynomial w.r.t.
|\phi|+|\mathfrak{R}|
.

Proof 5.40

By Definition
5.38
,
{\cal S}
is of the form
(\phi_{1}\sigma*\phi_{2}*\phi_{3})\curlywedge(\xi_{1}\wedge\xi_{2})\vdash_{\mathfrak{R}}^{V}\psi\curlywedge\top
, and satisfies Conditions
1
-
9
.
Note that since
\psi
is a predicate atom, we have
|\psi|\leq 1+\mathit{ar}_{\mathit{max}}(\mathfrak{R})\leq\kappa+1
and
\mathit{card}(\mathcal{V}(\psi))\leq\mathit{ar}_{\mathit{max}}(\mathfrak{R})\leq\kappa
.

We have
\mathit{len}(\phi_{1}*\phi_{2}*\phi_{3})=\mathit{len}(\phi_{1})+\mathit{len}(\phi_{2})+\mathit{len}(\phi_{3})\leq\mathit{len}(\phi_{1})+\kappa+1+\mathit{len}(\phi)\leq|\mathfrak{R}|+|\phi|+\kappa+1
,
thus

|\phi_{1}\sigma*\phi_{2}*\phi_{3}|\leq(\kappa+1)\cdot(|\mathfrak{R}|+|\phi|+\kappa+1)=O(|\phi|+|\mathfrak{R}|)
.
Since
\mathcal{V}(\zeta_{1})\cup\mathcal{V}(\zeta_{2})\subseteq\mathcal{V}(\phi_{1}\sigma*\phi_{2}*\phi_{3})\cup\mathcal{V}(\psi)
,
we deduce that
|\xi_{1}\wedge\xi_{2}|=O((|\phi|+|\mathfrak{R}|)^{2})
hence
|{\cal S}|=O((|\phi|+|\mathfrak{R}|)^{2})
.

Let
n=\mathit{card}(E)+|\phi|+\mathit{card}(\mathcal{C})+1
. Note that
n\leq\mathit{card}(E)+|\phi|+|\mathfrak{R}|+1
.
The number of possible predicate atoms
\psi
and
\alpha
is
at most

\mathit{card}(\mathcal{P})\cdot n^{\kappa}\leq|\mathfrak{R}|\cdot n^{\kappa}
. Similarly, the number of formulas

\phi_{2}
is at most
|\mathfrak{R}|^{\kappa+1}\cdot n^{(\kappa+1)^{2}}
.
Once
\alpha
is fixed, the number of formulas

\phi_{1}
cannot be greater than the number of rules in
\mathfrak{R}
, up to a renaming of variables.
Since
\mathit{card}(\mathit{dom}(\sigma))\leq\kappa
, there are at most

n^{2\cdot\kappa}

possible substitutions
\sigma
we only have to choose the set
\mathit{dom}(\sigma)
,
i.e., at most
\kappa
elements among the variables occurring in
\phi
or
\phi_{1}
, and the image of each variable in
\mathit{dom}(\sigma)

among the variables in
E
.
The number of formulas
\phi_{3}
is at most
\mathit{len}(\phi)^{2\cdot\kappa}

(since we only have to choose the formula
\eta_{1}
in
\phi
, as
\eta_{2}
is entirely determined by the choice of
\phi_{1},\phi_{2}
and
\eta_{1}
). We have
\mathcal{V}(\xi_{2})\subseteq\mathcal{V}(\psi)
thus

\mathit{card}(\mathcal{V}(\xi_{2}))\leq\kappa
, and there are
2^{\kappa^{2}}
possible
formulas
\xi_{2}
.
Since
V\subseteq\mathcal{V}(\psi)
, we have
\mathit{card}(V)\leq\kappa
and the number of possible sets
V
is
2^{\kappa}
.
Finally, the formulas
\xi_{0}
and
\xi_{1}
are fixed once
\phi
and
\phi_{1},\phi_{2},\phi_{3}
and
\sigma
are fixed.

Lemma
5.43
is used to prove that every sequent that is

I
-reducible and occurs in a proof tree is a companion of the left-hand side of the root of the proof tree. Together with
Lemma
5.39
and the result stated in Lemma
5.19
on the number of

I
-free descendants of a sequent, this will entail that the size of the proof tree is polynomial w.r.t. the size of the root.
We use the following proposition, which is an easy consequence of the definition of the rules.

Proposition 5.41

Let
{\cal S}_{i}=\lambda_{i}\vdash_{\mathfrak{R}}^{V_{i}}\gamma_{i}
(for
i=1,2
) be sequents.
If
{\cal S}_{2}
is a successor of
{\cal S}_{1}
, then

\mathit{alloc}(\lambda_{1})\cap\mathit{alloc}(\gamma_{1})\cap\mathit{alloc}(\lambda_{2})\subseteq\mathit{alloc}(\gamma_{2})
.

Proof 5.42

Assume that
x\in\mathit{alloc}(\lambda_{1})\cap\mathit{alloc}(\gamma_{1})\cap\mathit{alloc}(\lambda_{2})
and
x\not\in\mathit{alloc}(\gamma_{2})
.
It is easy to check, by inspection of the rules, that the only rule that can remove the variable
x
from

\mathit{alloc}(\gamma_{1})
is

S
(indeed,

I
deletes a predicate atom but introduce a points-to atom with the same root,
and the rules

R
and

C
cannot replace
x
as otherwise it would not occur in
\lambda_{2}
).
By definition of the rule,
{\cal S}_{1}
also has a successor

{\cal S}_{2}^{\prime}=\lambda_{2}^{\prime}\vdash_{\mathfrak{R}}^{V_{2}^{\prime}}\gamma_{2}^{\prime}
such that

x\in V_{2}^{\prime}
(since
x\in\mathit{alloc}(\lambda_{2})
) and
x\in\mathit{alloc}(\gamma_{2}^{\prime})
(since
x\not\in\mathit{alloc}(\gamma_{2})
). This entails that
{\cal S}_{2}^{\prime}
is an anti-axiom, contradicting Condition
1
in Definition
4.43
.

Lemma 5.43

Assume that

\mathit{ar}_{\mathit{max}}(\mathfrak{R})\leq\kappa
for
\kappa\in{\mathbb{N}}
, and let
{\cal S}=\phi\curlywedge\xi\vdash\psi\curlywedge\zeta
be an equality-free sequent.
Every

I
-reducible sequent occurring in a proof tree with root
{\cal S}

is a
\kappa
-companion of the left-hand side
\phi\curlywedge\xi
of
{\cal S}
.

Proof 5.44

Let
{\cal S}^{\prime}=\phi^{\prime}\curlywedge\xi^{\prime}\vdash_{\mathfrak{R}}^{V^{\prime}}\psi^{\prime}\curlywedge\zeta^{\prime}
be an

I
-reducible sequent occurring in a proof tree with root
{\cal S}
.
Let
{\cal S}_{1},\dots,{\cal S}_{n}
, be a path from
{\cal S}
to
{\cal S}^{\prime}
, where

{\cal S}_{1}={\cal S}
,
{\cal S}_{n}={\cal S}^{\prime}
and
{\cal S}_{i}=\lambda_{i}\vdash_{\mathfrak{R}}^{V_{i}}\gamma_{i}
for
i=1,\ldots,n
.
We check that all the conditions in Definition
5.38
are satisfied.
Note that by Condition
1
in Definition
4.43
,
{\cal S}^{\prime}
cannot be an axiom or an anti-axiom.

•

Right-Hand Side.

We first show that the right-hand side
\psi^{\prime}
is indeed a predicate atom. By Condition
2
in Definition
4.43
,
\zeta^{\prime}=\top
and
\psi^{\prime}
is a predicate atom, thus Condition
4
of Definition
5.38
holds.

•

Pure Formulas.

We then prove that the pure formulas
\xi^{\prime}
fulfill Conditions
1
,
2
and
3
.
If
\xi^{\prime}
contains an equality then the rule

R
applies, hence
{\cal S}^{\prime}
cannot be

I
-reducible because

R
has priority over

I
(Condition
6
in Definition
4.43
).
Thus
\xi^{\prime}
is a conjunction of disequations, and it can be written as

\xi_{0}\curlywedge\xi_{1}\wedge\xi_{2}
where

\xi_{0}
is the set of disequations in
\xi^{\prime}
between
terms of a sort distinct from
\mathtt{loc}
,

\mathcal{V}(\xi_{2})\subseteq\mathcal{V}(\psi^{\prime})\cap\mathcal{V}_{\mathtt{loc}}

and
\xi_{1}
only contains disequations of the form
u\not\approx v
(up to commutativity of
\not\approx
) with
u,v\in\mathcal{V}_{\mathtt{loc}}
and
u\not\in\mathcal{V}(\psi^{\prime})
.
Note that each disequation involves distinct terms because by hypothesis,
{\cal S}^{\prime}
is not an axiom.
Consider one such disequation
u\not\approx v
in
\xi_{1}
. If
u\not\in\mathcal{V}(\phi^{\prime})
then rule

W
applies by the second application condition of the rule, which is impossible since this rule has priority over

I
and
{\cal S}^{\prime}
would not be

I
-reducible. We deduce that
u\in\mathcal{V}(\phi^{\prime})
, and since
{\cal S}^{\prime}
is not an anti-axiom, we must have

u\in\mathit{alloc}(\phi^{\prime})
.
If
v\not\in\mathcal{V}(\phi^{\prime})\cup\mathcal{V}(\psi^{\prime})
or
v\in\mathit{alloc}(\phi^{\prime})\cup V^{\prime}
then rule

W
also applies (in the latter case, we have
\phi^{\prime}\triangleright_{V_{i}}u\not\approx v
by Condition
3
of Definition
4.13
). Thus
v\in\mathcal{V}(\phi^{\prime})\cup\mathcal{V}(\psi^{\prime})
and
v\not\in\mathit{alloc}(\phi^{\prime})\cup V
. This entails that

v\in\mathcal{V}(\psi^{\prime})
since otherwise
{\cal S}^{\prime}
would be an anti-axiom by Condition
5
of Definition
4.34
because
u\in\mathit{alloc}(\phi^{\prime})
, hence
v
is necessarily of sort
\mathtt{loc}
.
We therefore have
v\in{\cal V}^{\star}({\cal S}^{\prime})
and
u\in\mathit{alloc}(\phi^{\prime})\setminus\mathcal{V}(\psi^{\prime})
.
Conversely, if a disequation
u\not\approx v
with
v\in{\cal V}^{\star}({\cal S}^{\prime})
and
u\in\mathit{alloc}(\phi^{\prime})\setminus\mathcal{V}(\psi^{\prime})

does not occur in
\xi_{1}
then by Condition
4
in Definition
4.43
,

C
would be applicable, which is impossible.
Thus Conditions
2
and
3
in Definition
5.38
are satisfied.
By Proposition
4.23
(
5
) no rule introduces a disequation between terms of a sort distinct from
\mathtt{loc}
, all disequations in
\xi_{0}
must occur in

\xi
. Furthermore, such a disequation cannot be of the form
t\not\approx t
(otherwise
{\cal S}^{\prime}
would be an axiom as explained above)
and must contain a variable occurring in
\phi^{\prime}
or
\psi^{\prime}
(otherwise

W
would apply). Conversely, no rule can delete a disequation containing a variable in
(\mathcal{V}(\phi^{\prime})\cup\mathcal{V}(\psi^{\prime}))\setminus\mathcal{V}_{\mathtt{loc}}
from the left-hand side of the sequent.
Thus Condition
1
holds.

•

Non Allocated Variables.

We now check that Condition
5
applies, namely that
V^{\prime}\subseteq\mathcal{V}(\psi^{\prime})
.
Assume, for the sake of contradiction, that
V^{\prime}
contains a variable
x\not\in\mathcal{V}(\psi^{\prime})
. Since
{\cal S}^{\prime}
is not an anti-axiom, we have
V^{\prime}\cap(\mathcal{V}(\phi^{\prime})\setminus\mathcal{V}(\psi^{\prime}))=\emptyset
, hence
x\not\in\mathcal{V}(\phi^{\prime})
. If
x\in\mathcal{V}(\xi^{\prime})
then

W

applies,
and otherwise

V
applies. In both cases,
{\cal S}^{\prime}
cannot be

I
-reducible (since

W
and

V
have priority over

I
), which contradicts our assumption. Thus Condition
5
holds.

•

Substitution
\sigma
.
We now define the substitution
\sigma
, and we show that Condition
8
holds.
Let
i
be the least number such that the right-hand side
\gamma_{i}
of
{\cal S}_{i}
is a spatial atom (
i
must exist since
n
fulfills this condition, by Condition
2
in Definition
4.43
), and let
V^{\star}={\cal V}^{\star}({\cal S}_{i})
.
Note that
\gamma_{i}
must be a predicate atom.
Indeed, the right-hand side of
\gamma_{n}=\lambda_{n}\vdash_{\mathfrak{R}}^{V_{n}}\gamma_{n}
is a predicate atom since
{\cal S}_{n}
is

I
-reducible, and by Condition
6
of Proposition
4.23
, the only rule that can introduce a predicate atom to the right-hand side of a premise is

I
, which by Condition
2
in Definition
4.43
, applies only if the right-hand side of the conclusion is a predicate atom.
Thus
\gamma_{j}
cannot be a points-to atom.
By Proposition
5.5
we have

{\cal V}^{\star}({\cal S}_{j})\subseteq V^{\star}
, for all
j=i,\dots,n
, and
\mathit{card}(V^{\star})\leq\mathit{ar}_{\mathit{max}}(\mathfrak{R})\leq\kappa
since
V^{\star}\subseteq\mathcal{V}({\cal S}_{i})
and
\gamma_{i}
is a predicate atom.
Let
I\subseteq\{1,\dots,n\}
be the set of indices
j
such that rule

C
applies on
{\cal S}_{j}
, and replaces a
variable
x_{j}
by a variable
y_{j}
(i.e., such that
{\cal S}_{j+1}
is a left premise of an application of

C
on
{\cal S}_{j}
with variables
x_{j}
and
y_{j}
).
By Condition
4
of Definition
4.43
,

C
applies only if the right-hand side of the conclusion is a predicate atom,
thus we must have
I\subseteq\{i,\dots,n\}
.
Let
\sigma=\sigma_{n}
, where

\sigma_{0}=\id
,
\sigma_{j+1}=\sigma_{j}\{x_{j+1}\leftarrow y_{j+1}\}
if
j+1\in I
and
\sigma_{j+1}=\sigma_{j}
otherwise.
By Condition
4
in Definition
4.43
,
for all
j\in I
, we have
y_{j}\in{\cal V}^{\star}({\cal S}_{j})\subseteq{\cal V}^{\star}({\cal S}_{i})=V^{\star}
. Also,

y_{j}\not\in{\cal V}^{\star}({\cal S}_{j+1})
because
x_{j}
must be allocated in the left-hand side of
{\cal S}_{j}
, hence, after the replacement,
y_{j}
is allocated in the left-hand side of
{\cal S}_{j+1}
. Thus by Proposition
5.5
,
y_{j}\not\in{\cal V}^{\star}({\cal S}_{j^{\prime}})
, for all
j^{\prime}>j
, which entails that
the

y_{j}
for
j\in I
are pairwise distinct.
Therefore,
\mathit{card}(I)\leq\mathit{card}(V^{\star})\leq\kappa
, hence
\mathit{card}(\mathit{dom}(\sigma))\leq\kappa
and Condition
8
is satisfied.

•

Unfolded Atom.

Now we define the unfolded predicate atom
\alpha
and we prove that Condition
6
holds.
If rule

U
is applied on a sequent
{\cal S}_{k}
and
{\cal S}^{\prime}
is an

I
-free descendant of
{\cal S}_{k}
, then we denote by
\alpha
the predicate atom on which

U
is applied, and by
\phi_{1}\curlywedge\chi
the formula occurring in
{\cal S}_{k+1}
such that
\alpha\Leftarrow_{\mathfrak{R}}\phi_{1}\curlywedge\chi
.
If no such application of

U
exists then we simply set
\phi_{1}=\mathit{emp}
.
Note that if
k
and
\alpha
exist then they must be unique, since
we assumed that
{\cal S}^{\prime}
is an

I
-free descendant of
{\cal S}_{k}

and by Corollary
5.12

there must be an application of

I
along the path between any two applications of

U
.
Also, by Condition
3
in Definition
4.43
,

\gamma_{k}
is a spatial
atom and
\mathit{root}(\alpha)=\mathit{root}(\gamma_{k})
. By Proposition
5.8
, the only rules that can be applied on
{\cal S}_{k+1},\dots,{\cal S}_{n-1}
are

W
,

C
, or

V
. Each of these rules only affects the right-hand side of its premise
by instantiating it with
\sigma

thus
\mathit{root}(\alpha)\sigma=\mathit{root}(\psi^{\prime})
.
First consider the case where
k
exists. Since the rules in
\mathfrak{R}
are
\mathtt{P}
-rules,
\lambda_{k+1}
must contain a points-to atom. By Condition
3
in Definition
4.43
, the root of this atom must be the same as that of
\gamma_{k}=\gamma_{k+1}
. Furthermore,
\lambda_{k+1}
contains no equation, since otherwise this equation would occur in
\lambda_{k}
, rule

R
would be applicable on
{\cal S}_{k}
and this rule has priority over

U
. Therefore,
{\cal S}_{k+1}
is quasi-

I
-reducible.
By Proposition
5.8
, this entails that only the rules

W
,

V
or

C
may be applied along the path

{\cal S}_{k+1},\dots,{\cal S}_{n}
. These rules either do not affect spatial formulas, or
uniformly replace a variable
x_{j}
(for
j\in I
) by
y_{j}=x_{j}\sigma_{j}
(hence eventually, every variable
x
is replaced by
x\sigma
).
Thus
\phi^{\prime}
is of the form
\phi_{1}\sigma*\phi_{1}^{\prime}
.
It is clear that the previous assertion trivially holds in the case where
k
does not exist by letting
\phi_{1}=\mathit{emp}
. Thus Condition
6
holds.

•

Spatial Formula.

We now prove that the spatial formula
\phi_{1}^{\prime}
is of the required form and fulfills Condition
7
. The formula
\phi_{1}^{\prime}
may be written as

\phi_{2}*\phi_{3}*\phi_{4}
, where:

–

\mathit{alloc}(\phi_{2})\subseteq(V^{\star}\cup\mathit{alloc}(\psi^{\prime}))
,

–

\mathit{alloc}(\phi_{3})\subseteq\mathcal{V}(\phi)\setminus(V^{\star}\cup\mathit{alloc}(\psi^{\prime}))
,

–

\mathit{alloc}(\phi_{4})\cap(\mathcal{V}(\phi)\cup V^{\star}\cup\mathit{alloc}(\psi^{\prime}))=\emptyset
.

If
\phi_{2}
contains two atoms with the same root then
\phi^{\prime}
is heap-unsatisfiable and
{\cal S}^{\prime}
is an axiom.
Thus
\mathit{len}(\phi_{2})\leq\mathit{card}(V^{\star})+1\leq\kappa+1
and Condition
7
holds.

The only rule that can add new predicate symbols to a premise is

U
, replacing an atom

\alpha^{\prime}
by a formula
\lambda
such that
\alpha^{\prime}\Leftarrow_{\mathfrak{R}}\lambda
.
Since the rules in
\mathfrak{R}
are
\mathtt{P}
-rules, the root of every predicate atom in
\lambda
must be a fresh variable
y
, not occurring in
\mathcal{V}(\phi)
. If
y\in\mathit{dom}(\sigma)
then
y
is eventually replaced by some variable in
V^{\star}
, hence
the corresponding predicate atom from
\phi_{1}^{\prime}
is in
\phi_{2}
.
If
y\not\in\mathit{dom}(\sigma)
, then since this variable is never replaced, the corresponding atom must occur in
\phi_{4}
by construction.
This entails that all the atoms in
\phi_{3}
must be obtained from atoms in
\phi
after application of the substitution
\sigma
, i.e., that

\phi\sigma=\phi_{3}*\eta
for some formula
\eta
, up to AC.
The formula
\eta
can be written as
\eta_{1}*\eta_{2}
where

\mathit{alloc}(\eta_{1})\subseteq\mathcal{V}(\psi^{\prime})\cup V^{\star}
and

\mathit{alloc}(\eta_{2})\cap(\mathcal{V}(\psi^{\prime})\cup V^{\star})=\emptyset
.
This entails that
\mathit{len}(\eta_{1})\leq 2\cdot\kappa
, since
\mathit{card}(\mathcal{V}(\psi^{\prime}))\leq\kappa
and

\mathit{card}(V^{\star})\leq\kappa
.

We prove that necessarily
\phi_{4}=\mathit{emp}
.
Assume for the sake of contradiction that
\phi_{4}
contains an atom
\alpha^{\prime}
, and let
u=\mathit{root}(\alpha^{\prime})
. By definition of
\phi_{4}
,
u\not\in\mathcal{V}(\phi)\cup V^{\star}\cup\mathit{alloc}(\psi^{\prime})
.
The variable
u
must have been introduced by an application of rule

U
on some sequent

{\cal S}_{m}
, as

U
is the only rule that can introduce new variables to the left-hand side of a sequent. By Proposition
5.10
,
{\cal S}_{m+1}
is quasi-

I
-reducible, and we must have
m\not=k
(if
k
exists), since otherwise
\alpha^{\prime}
would occur in
\phi_{1}\sigma
.
Since the rules in
\mathfrak{R}
are
\mathtt{P}
-rules, we have
u\in{\cal V}_{\mapsto}({\cal S}_{m+1})
.
Since
m\not=k
, by Corollary
5.12
, there exists
m^{\prime}
such that

m<m^{\prime}

and
{\cal S}_{m^{\prime}}
is

I
-reducible, i.e., rule

I
is applied on
{\cal S}_{m^{\prime}}
.
Let
m^{\prime}
be the minimal number satisfying this property.
The path
{\cal S}_{m+1},\dots,{\cal S}_{m^{\prime}}
only contains
applications of

W
,

V
and

C
, and
u\in{\cal V}_{\mapsto}({\cal S}_{m^{\prime}})
by Proposition
5.8
.
Note that if
k
exists then necessarily
m^{\prime}<k
, moreover,
none of these rules can introduce new variables to the right-hand side of a sequent. In particular, variable
u
, which is a fresh variable that does not occur in
\gamma_{m}
, cannot occur on the right-hand side
\gamma_{m^{\prime}}
of
{\cal S}_{m^{\prime}}
.
Let
\theta
denote the substitution used in the application of

I
on
{\cal S}_{m^{\prime}}
.
As
u\in{\cal V}_{\mapsto}({\cal S}_{m^{\prime}})
, there exists a variable
z\in\mathit{dom}(\theta)
such that
z\theta=u
.
Since the rules in
\mathfrak{R}
are
\mathtt{P}
-rules, this entails that
u\in\mathit{alloc}(\gamma_{m^{\prime}+1})
.
Consequently,
u\in\mathit{alloc}(\lambda_{m^{\prime}+1})\cap\mathit{alloc}(\gamma_{m^{\prime}+1})
and by (repeatedly) applying Proposition
5.41
, we deduce that

u\in\mathit{alloc}(\psi^{\prime})
(since by hypothesis
u\in\mathit{alloc}(\phi_{4})\subseteq\mathit{alloc}(\phi^{\prime})
), which contradicts the definition of
\phi_{4}
.

•

Reachability Conditions.

Finally, we show that Condition
9
holds, i.e., that
\eta_{2}
contains exactly the atoms
\beta
in
\phi_{3}*\eta_{2}
such that
\mathit{root}(\psi^{\prime})\not\rightarrow_{\phi_{1}\sigma*\phi_{2}*\phi_{3}*\eta_{2}}\mathit{root}(\beta)
.
Assume that
\phi_{3}
contains an atom
\beta
such that
\mathit{root}(\psi^{\prime})\not\rightarrow_{\phi_{1}\sigma*\phi_{2}*\phi_{3}*\eta_{2}}\mathit{root}(\beta)
.
Since
\phi^{\prime}=\phi_{1}\sigma*\phi_{2}*\phi_{3}
, we get
\mathit{root}(\psi^{\prime})\not\rightarrow_{\phi^{\prime}}\mathit{root}(\beta)
,
\mathit{root}(\beta)\in\mathit{alloc}(\phi^{\prime})

and

\mathit{root}(\beta)\not\in\mathit{alloc}(\psi^{\prime})
(because
\mathit{root}(\beta)\not=\mathit{root}(\psi^{\prime})
and
\mathit{alloc}(\psi^{\prime})=\{\mathit{root}(\psi^{\prime})\}
), thus
{\cal S}^{\prime}
is an anti-axiom.
Conversely, assume that
\eta_{2}
contains an atom
\beta
such that
\mathit{root}(\psi^{\prime})\rightarrow_{\phi_{1}\sigma*\phi_{2}*\phi_{3}*\eta_{2}}\mathit{root}(\beta)
.
Note that
\mathit{root}(\beta)\not\in\mathcal{V}(\psi^{\prime})\cup V^{\star}
, by definition of
\eta_{2}
, which entails that
\mathit{root}(\beta)\not\in\mathit{codom}(\sigma)
.
If
\mathit{root}(\beta)\not\in\mathit{alloc}(\phi^{\prime})
, then by considering the first variable
y\not\in\mathit{alloc}(\phi^{\prime})=\mathit{alloc}(\phi_{1}\sigma*\phi_{2}*\phi_{3})
along the path from
\mathit{root}(\psi^{\prime})

to
\mathit{root}(\beta)
, we have

y\in\mathcal{V}(\phi^{\prime})
,
y\not\in\mathit{alloc}(\phi^{\prime})
and

y\in\mathit{alloc}(\eta_{2})
, hence
y\not\in\mathcal{V}(\psi^{\prime})
. Thus
{\cal S}^{\prime}
is an anti-axiom, contradicting our hypothesis.
Therefore,
\mathit{root}(\beta)\in\mathit{alloc}(\phi^{\prime})=\mathit{alloc}(\phi_{1}\sigma)\cup\mathit{alloc}(\phi_{2})\cup\mathit{alloc}(\phi_{3})
. We distinguish the three cases:

–

Assume that
\mathit{root}(\beta)\in\mathit{alloc}(\phi_{1}\sigma)
i.e., that
k
exists,
\phi_{1}\neq\mathit{emp}
and
\alpha\Leftarrow_{\mathfrak{R}}\phi_{1}\curlywedge\chi
. Since
\mathit{root}(\beta)\not\in\mathit{codom}(\sigma)
, we also have
\mathit{root}(\beta)\in\mathit{alloc}(\phi_{1})
. Since the rules in
\mathfrak{R}
are
\mathtt{P}
-rules, the variables in
\mathit{alloc}(\phi_{1})
are either the root of
\alpha
or fresh variables. Now
\mathit{root}(\alpha)\sigma
must be distinct from
\mathit{root}(\beta)
, since
\mathit{root}(\beta)\not\in\mathcal{V}(\psi^{\prime})
and
\mathit{root}(\alpha)\sigma=\mathit{root}(\psi^{\prime})
. Furthermore, all fresh variables are necessarily distinct from
\mathit{root}(\beta)
. Indeed, they cannot occur in
\phi
, by definition, and
\mathit{root}(\beta)\subseteq\mathit{alloc}(\phi)
, as
\mathit{root}(\beta)\subseteq\mathit{alloc}(\eta_{2})\subseteq\mathit{alloc}(\eta)\subseteq\mathit{alloc}(\phi\sigma)
and
\mathit{root}(\beta)\not\in\mathit{codom}(\sigma)
. Thus this case cannot occur.

–

If
\mathit{root}(\beta)\in\mathit{alloc}(\phi_{2})
, then
\mathit{root}(\beta)\in V^{\star}\cup\mathit{alloc}(\psi^{\prime})
(by definition of
\phi_{2}
), which contradicts the fact that
\mathit{root}(\beta)\not\in\mathcal{V}(\psi^{\prime})\cup V^{\star}
.

–

If
\mathit{root}(\beta)\in\mathit{alloc}(\phi_{3})
, then
\mathit{root}(\beta)
occurs twice in
\mathit{alloc}(\phi\sigma)
because
\phi\sigma=\phi_{3}*\eta_{1}*\eta_{2}
; hence, it also occurs twice in
\mathit{alloc}(\phi)
because
\mathit{root}(\beta)\not\in\mathit{codom}(\sigma)
. This entails that
\phi
is heap-unsatisfiable, a contradiction.

Thus we get a contradiction, which entails that Condition
9
in Definition
4.43
is satisfied.

We now introduce a restricted form of variable renaming
3
3

3

Note that we cannot assume that sequents are taken modulo variable renaming, since identity would then be difficult to test (it would be equivalent to the well-known graph isomorphism problem).
. The definition is useful to straightforwardly detect cycles in a proof tree, which is essential for efficiency.
For each sort
{\mathtt{s}}
