, we consider infinite (fixed) sequences of pairwise distinct variables of sort
{\mathtt{s}}
:
x_{i}^{\mathtt{s}}
, with
i\in{\mathbb{N}}
(not occurring in the root sequent). A sequent
{\cal S}^{\prime}
occurring in a proof tree with root
{\cal S}
is
normalized
if, for every variable
y
of sort
{\mathtt{s}}
occurring in
\mathcal{V}({\cal S}^{\prime})\setminus\mathcal{V}({\cal S})
, there exists
i\leq|{\cal S}^{\prime}|
such that

y=x_{i}^{\mathtt{s}}
. It is clear that every sequent
{\cal S}^{\prime}
can be reduced in polynomial time to a normalized sequent. Indeed, it suffices to rename all the variables
y_{1},\dots,y_{n}
of sorts
{\mathtt{s}}_{1},\dots,{\mathtt{s}}_{n}
occurring in
{\cal S}^{\prime}
but not in
{\cal S}
by the variables

x_{1}^{{\mathtt{s}}_{1}},\dots,x_{n}^{{\mathtt{s}}_{n}}
, respectively. As
n
cannot be greater than the size of
{\cal S}^{\prime}
, the obtained sequent is normalized.
Such an operation is called a
normalization
.

Lemma 5.45

Assume that

\mathit{ar}_{\mathit{max}}(\mathfrak{R})\leq\kappa
, for some fixed
\kappa\in{\mathbb{N}}
.
For every sequent
{\cal S}
, the number of sequents occurring in a proot tree with root
{\cal S}
is polynomial w.r.t.
|{\cal S}|+|\mathfrak{R}|
, up to normalization.

Proof 5.46

By definition, every descendant of
{\cal S}
is either an

I
-free descendant of
{\cal S}

or an

I
-free descendant of some

I
-reducible sequent
{\cal S}^{\prime}
.
By Condition
6
in Definition
4.43
,

R
applies with the highest priority on
{\cal S}
, eventually yielding a (unique) sequent equality-free
{\cal S}^{\prime\prime}
of the form
\phi\curlywedge\xi\vdash_{\mathfrak{R}}^{V}\psi\curlywedge\zeta
, with
|{\cal S}^{\prime\prime}|\leq|{\cal S}|
and
\mathcal{V}(\phi)\subseteq\mathcal{V}({\cal S})
.
By Lemma
5.43
, every

I
-free sequent
{\cal S}^{\prime}
is a companion of
of
\phi\curlywedge\xi
.
By Lemma
5.39
, the number of such sequents
{\cal S}^{\prime}
, after normalization (w.r.t.
{\cal S}^{\prime\prime}
), is polynomial w.r.t.
|\phi|+|\mathfrak{R}|
(hence w.r.t.
|{\cal S}|+|\mathfrak{R}|
).
Indeed, it is clear that the normalized form of
{\cal S}^{\prime}
is also a companion of

\phi\curlywedge\xi
and by definition it only contains variables in
\mathcal{V}({\cal S}^{\prime\prime})\cup\{x_{i}^{\mathtt{s}}\mid i\leq|{\cal S}^{\prime}|\}
. Furthermore,
|{\cal S}^{\prime}|
is polynomial w.r.t.
|{\cal S}|+|\mathfrak{R}|
, thus the same property holds for
\mathit{card}(\mathcal{V}({\cal S}^{\prime}))
.
Then the proof follows immediately from Lemma
5.19
.

Putting everything together, we derive the main result of the paper:

Theorem 5.47

The validity problem for sequents
\lambda\vdash_{\mathfrak{R}}^{V}\gamma
, where
\mathfrak{R}
is a
\mathtt{loc}
-deterministic set of
\mathtt{P}
-rules with

\mathit{ar}_{\mathit{max}}(\mathfrak{R})\leq\kappa

and

\mathit{record}_{\mathit{max}}(\mathfrak{R})\leq\kappa
,
for some fixed number
\kappa
,
is in
PTime
.

Proof 5.48

By Theorems
5.21
and
5.36
,
\lambda\vdash_{\mathfrak{R}}^{V}\gamma
is valid iff
it admits a fully expanded proof tree.
We first apply inductively the inference rules in all possible ways, starting with the initial sequent
\lambda\vdash_{\mathfrak{R}}^{V}\gamma
and proceeding with all the descendants of
\lambda\vdash_{\mathfrak{R}}^{V}\gamma
, until we obtain either an axiom or a sequent that has already been considered, up to normalization.
By Lemma
5.45
, the number of such descendants (up to normalization) is polynomial w.r.t.
|{\cal S}|+|\mathfrak{R}|
, thus it suffices to show that each inference step can be performed in polynomial time. This can be established by an inspection of the rules. We first observe that the set of all possible rule applications can be computed in polynomial time w.r.t. the size of the conclusion (in particular, both the number of premises and their size are always polynomial w.r.t. the size of the conclusion): this is straightforward to check for all rules except for

S
, for which the result stems from Proposition
4.47
.
Also, it is straightforward to verify that the application conditions of the rules can be tested in polynomial time.

Afterwards, it suffices to compute the set of provable sequents among those that are reachable from the initial one and check whether
\lambda\vdash_{\mathfrak{R}}^{V}\gamma
occurs in this set.
Since infinite proof trees are allowed, we actually compute the complement of this set, i.e., the set of sequents that are not provable. This can be done in polynomial time, using a straightforward fixpoint algorithm, by computing the least set of sequents
{\cal S}
such that, for all rule applications with conclusion
{\cal S}
, at least one of the premises is not provable. More precisely, a sequent is not provable if it has no successor (no rule can be applied on it), or more generally, if for all possible rule applications, at least one of the premises is not provable.

Note that, in order to apply rule

S
on a sequent that is not narrow, it is necessary to check that the left premise is valid, and this must be done by recursively applying the proof procedure. This is feasible because the left premise is always narrow (since its right-hand side is a predicate atom).
Consequently, the computation of valid sequents must be performed in two steps, first for narrow sequents, then for those that are not narrow, as the application of the rules on non narrow sequents depends on the validity of narrow sequents.
By Proposition
4.44
all the descendants of narrow sequents are also narrow, hence the converse does not hold, and the procedure is well-founded.

6
Conclusion

A proof procedure has been devised for checking entailments between Separation Logic formulas
with spatial predicates denoting recursive data structures, for a new class of inductive rules, called
\mathtt{loc}
-deterministic. The soundness and completeness of the calculus have been established.
The conditions imposed on the data structures are stronger than those considered in
[
14
,
15
]
but on the other hand
the procedure terminates in polynomial time, provided the arity of the predicates is bounded.
Both the considered fragment and the proof procedure are very different from previously known polynomial-time decision procedures for testing entailments
[
8
,
5
]
. In particular, the considered formulas may contain several non-built-in and non-compositional predicates (in the sense of
[
12
]
), and the structures may contain unallocated nodes, denoting data (with no predicate other than equality).
In addition, several lower bounds have been established for the entailment problem, showing that any relaxing of the proposed conditions makes the problem untractable.

Future work includes the implementation of the procedure and the evaluation of its practical performance.
The combination with theory reasoning (to reason on data stored inside the structures) will also be considered.
In particular, it would be interesting to define a fragment that encompasses both
the
\mathtt{loc}
-deterministic
\mathtt{P}
-rules considered in the present paper and the SHLIDe fragment defined in
[
19
]
, as the conditions defining both classes of rules are very different and non comparable, as evidenced by the examples and explanations given in the introduction of the paper.
